And so the impact of **changing useful life** on **lease classification** has **no impact on that cash paid for PP&E** — and so I wouldn't think about that change as impacting the guidance we were able to give on free cash flow."*** ➜ **Useful-life and lease-classification changes move reported D&A, reported capex classification and reported operating margin — they do NOT move the cash line the FCF guide is struck on. Any model that "fixes" MSFT FCF for the useful-life change is double-adjusting.** (Microsoft IR call — Mary, 2026-08-13)
- ⚠️🔴 **A DOUBLE-COUNT TO REMOVE, IDENTIFIED IN THE SAME CALL AND FLAGGED HERE BECAUSE IT AFFECTS FORWARD MARGIN/CASH MODELS ON THIS PAGE'S LARGEST NAME: the cessation of the [[MSFT]]→[[OPENAI]] revenue share was logged on this wiki (07-30, UBS callback) as an **FY27** tailwind. IR dates it EARLIER: *"we stopped paying out a revenue share to OpenAI. Right to think that that was **coming out of COGS**. So more of a **margin impact** than on the revenue side. **I would think of it as being embedded in what you saw in our fiscal year Q4 or calendar year Q2**."*** ➜ **It is ALREADY IN THE REPORTED F4Q26 NUMBERS. Carrying it forward as a future tailwind double-counts it. Logged as a correction, not a new datapoint.** (Microsoft IR call — Mary, 2026-08-13)
- **THE POWER/SHELL CONSTRAINT BEHIND THE SPEND — the reason capex converts to revenue on a lag — is filed in full on [ai-datacenter-power](ai-datacenter-power.md) (the constraint is a *"powered up data center shell"*, with management ruling out BOTH servers and energy availability; constrained *"at least through the end of the calendar year"*; +2GW FY25, ~1GW in each of the last three quarters, *"upwards of three added in this year"*, *"halfway through"* a two-year doubling).** ⚠️ **Do NOT derive a total footprint from those adds — IR volunteered that it has never disclosed the base.** Not duplicated here. (Microsoft IR call — Mary, 2026-08-13)

- 🔴 **AN INDEPENDENT PUTS THE THREE-HYPERSCALER 2026 NUMBER AT ~$600B — AND ON THIS PAGE'S OWN TABLE THAT IS A STREET-HIGH PRINT, NOT A CONSENSUS ONE.** *"[Amazon, Microsoft and Google] together will spend around **$600B in CapEx this year** and have all come to the same conclusion: **they do not want to keep paying Nvidia's margin forever**"* (UncoverAlpha, "Nvidia and the Hyperscalers: High Stakes Poker Game", 2026-08-13). **RECONCILIATION AGAINST THE STATE-OF-PLAY TABLE ABOVE (simple sums of numbers already on this page, MSFT+AMZN+GOOGL 2026E, no new estimate): consensus mean $158+$199+$187 = **~$544bn**; street-high $193+$208+$192 = **~$593bn**; this page's house line $170+$200+$180 = **~$550bn**.** ➜ **~$600B is ~10% above consensus and effectively AT street-high. Carry it as a directional third-party mark, NOT as a revision — the source is an independent Substack synthesis with no model behind the figure.** [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Nvidia_and_the_Hyperscalers_High_Stakes_Poker_Game.html)
- **THE ASSET-LIFE SPLIT INSIDE THAT SPEND, which is the mechanic behind the "option to stop" framing already logged on this page (08-02) and is the part worth adding: on the Q2 calls the buyers *"all committed to **long-lead-time CapEx (land, shells, power, cooling — assets that last 15-25 years) years in advance**, but said they are going to buy **the chips, the 'short-lived assets,' only months before a data center comes online**. If demand is there, you buy them; if not, you don't buy the chips"* — *"**if the demand environment changes, you just slow down what is, in fact, the largest component**"* (Amy Hood, Microsoft, as quoted); *"**Google will keep investing as long as we see an attractive return on that investment**"* (Anat Ashkenazi, as quoted); with Jassy *"talking… about **different capital cycles** for data center land, power vs chips."*** ➜ **The buy-side consequence: the two halves of hyperscaler capex have completely different cancellability. The long-lived half is effectively committed and is the floor under the power/shell complex; the silicon half is a rolling, months-ahead decision. A capex "cut" headline is therefore almost always a cut to the SILICON half — which is why merchant-GPU order visibility is the fragile line item and why internal ASIC roadmaps (set annually, not quarterly) are the more stable one. Cross-filed [custom-asic-tpu](custom-asic-tpu.md).** ⚠️ **These quotes are RELAYED by an independent Substack, not taken from the call transcripts — treat as corroboration of the 08-02 block, not as a primary quote source.** (UncoverAlpha, 2026-08-13)

- 🔴 **THE FULL CY30 COMPOSITION TABLE — the most granular decomposition of what the capex BUYS that this page has carried** (BofA Securities · Vivek Arya / Duksan Jang / Michael Mani / Liam Pharr, "Rise of the agents: raising CPU TAM (again) to $210bn", 2026-08-12). Data center systems TAM **$505.6bn (2025) → $803.0bn (2026E) → $2,160.6bn (CY30E), +28% CAGR CY26-30**, against overall IT spend growing **+8% CAGR to $8,726bn**. Within it: **AI DC systems $272.7bn (2025) → $563.8bn (2026E) → $1,774.7bn (CY30E), +33% CAGR**, and **non-AI DC systems $384bn at +13% CAGR**. The CY30 stack: **AI servers $1.4Tn (~75-80%) · AI networking $310bn (~15-20%) · storage $85bn (~5%)**; inside servers, **AI accelerators ~$1.1Tn (from $197bn CY25 and $379bn CY26) · HBM $277bn (~25% of accelerator spend, "though likely stabilizes around that range as memory tier broadens") · server CPU $210bn (of which AI CPU ~$180bn, +44% CAGR from $42bn CY26) · AI connectivity $109bn from $41bn CY26 (copper +43% CAGR to $22bn, optical +25% to $87bn)**. [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Vivek_on_CPU.html)
- ⚠️⚠️ **TWO BASIS FLAGS BEFORE ANY OF THAT TOUCHES THE CAPEX TABLE ABOVE. (1) THIS IS A SYSTEMS TAM — VENDOR REVENUE FOR DATA CENTER SYSTEMS — NOT HYPERSCALER CAPEX. It excludes land, shells and most of the power stack (exactly the "long-lead" half of the spend described above) and it INCLUDES non-hyperscaler buyers (neoclouds, enterprises, sovereigns, labs). It is a COMPOSITION reference, not a level to compare against the $544-593bn 2026E table. This is the same error class as the OMDIA "CSP hardware capex" denominator already flagged on this page (08-09) and the gross-basis GCP trap (08-07). (2) NAME COLLISION: BofA's "$210bn" is the CY30 SERVER CPU TAM. This page separately carries "~$210bn" as BofA's own [[META]] 2028E CAPEX path ($145bn'26 → $185bn'27 → ~$210bn'28). Two unrelated numbers from the same house — do not cross-quote.**
- **THE COMPOSITION SHIFT ITSELF, which is the reason to carry the table: BofA raises CY30E server CPU TAM to **$210bn+ from ~$170bn** and lifts CPUs to **~10% of DC systems TAM from ~7% in the CY24-25 training era**, on the argument that **agentic inference pushes the CPU:GPU ratio from ~1:4 (training) → ~1:2 (AI inference) → ~1:1 (agentic)** as CPUs become *"the orchestration control plane"* — and, critically for this page, that this is **EXPANSION rather than SUBSTITUTION**: CPUs are *"additive to overall system TAM… layer[ing] new CPU dense infrastructure **alongside (not instead of) XPUs**."* ➜ **If BofA is right, a rising CPU share of the bill of materials is NOT a bearish signal for accelerator dollars — it is an increase in the total dollars a given GW of demand converts into. That direction matters: this page's recurring bear case is that capex growth must decelerate because the accelerator line cannot compound; a broadening BOM is one of the few mechanisms by which the level keeps rising while accelerator growth slows (BofA has XPU UNITS growing only +10.9% CAGR CY26-30 against AI CPU units +34.3%).** ⚠️ **Single-house forecast, and the CPU-only-rack demand it rests on is nascent — the agentic-node line is $0 before CY26E and $6.4bn in CY26E. The demand-side detail is filed on [tokenmaxxing](tokenmaxxing.md).** (BofA · Vivek Arya, 2026-08-12)

- **THE ROIC LEG — RE-AFFIRMED, NOT RE-MARKED** (Morgan Stanley · Brian Nowak / Julian Herrera / Gregory Gao / Nikhil Javeri / Kavya Narayanan, "Internet | North America — How Could Open-Weight Models Impact GenAI ROIC?", 2026-08-12, Industry View Attractive). MS re-ran its GenAI ROIC frameworks with open-weight models explicitly in the frame and published **the same bands as its 27-Jul note: hyperscaler IaaS ROIC 23-39% on 1GW of GB300 ($7-10/hr), and ~20-60% ROIC on 1GW of OWNED GB300 at ~$1.75/mn tokens** — concluding that *"**compute is the scarce resource**"*, that cheaper models mean *"substantially more volume at lower per token or GW unit economics"* where *"**EBIT dollar growth matters more than percentage margins**"*, and that investors should back *"the hyperscaler (or owner of compute) that has the **lowest cost to serve**"*. **OW [[AMZN]] and [[GOOG]]**; PTs **META $775 / AMZN $335 / GOOGL $400**. ⚠️ **THIS IS A DIFFERENT NOTE FROM THE MS "3 STATES OF THE WORLD" ITEM IN THE 08-12 BLOCK BELOW (Byrd/Hao/Nowak, "The Model Race Gets More Complicated", 2026-08-12) — same team lead, same day, different publications; do not merge them.** ⚠️ **Re-affirmation is NOT independent corroboration of the 07-27 grid.** Full framework detail on [tokenmaxxing](tokenmaxxing.md). [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/INTERNET_20260812_2000.html)

## Recent signals (Aug 12, /run-inbox — added later the same day) — MS FRAMES THE MODEL-ARCHITECTURE QUESTION AS A CAPEX **COMPOSITION** RISK, NOT A LEVEL RISK — AND ON-SITE POWER IS THE ONLY SCENARIO-INVARIANT LAYER

**MS's "3 States of the World" — the capex-relevant framing** (Morgan Stanley · Stephen Byrd / Sabrina Hao / Brian Nowak, "The Model Race Gets More Complicated: Chinese Model Pricing & Open-Weights Adoption", 2026-08-12). MS frames the open-vs-closed-weights question as having implications for *"1) token costs, 2) Jevons Paradox / the speed of enterprise adoption, **3) hyperscaler capex**, and 4) what segments of the tech stack benefit most,"* and maps beneficiaries across three scenarios (Exhibit 2). [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/GLOBAL_20260812_0401.html)
- **Scenario 1 — closed models win.** A small number of well-capitalised labs; workloads stay **concentrated in hyperscaler clouds**; training demand stays highly compute-intensive; **compute cost stays HIGHER than in the open case given market oligopoly**; *"the massive electricity demand of training superclusters further exacerbates the demand for **reliable, GW scale power**."*
- **Scenario 2 — hybrid.** Closed frontier models take complex reasoning/agentic work while open and smaller models take high-volume, cost-sensitive and domain-specific tasks; deployment spans **public cloud, private cloud, on-prem and edge**; closed models keep pricing power but competition *"limits excess returns."*
- **Scenario 3 — open models win.** API prices fall, adoption broadens per Jevons, innovation shifts from pre-training to **fine-tuning / inference optimisation / agents**, and AI decentralises into *"substantial growth in **private data centers, sovereign clouds, on-prem systems and edge devices**."*
- 🔴 **THE STRUCTURALLY USEFUL OBSERVATION FOR THIS PAGE: the on-site power group (BE, INIO, SEI, [[WMB]], LBRT) is the ONLY layer of the stack MS places IDENTICALLY in all three columns.** Cloud providers, semis, SaaS, optical/networking, colocation, on-prem and edge devices all reshuffle between scenarios. ➜ **If MS's framework is right, the model-architecture debate is a COMPOSITION risk to capex, not a LEVEL risk — the dollars move around the stack rather than disappearing, and power is scenario-invariant.** Two further extractions worth carrying: **[[NVDA]] is the only semi present in all three columns — [[AVGO]] appears only under closed-wins**; and **[[DELL]] plus the on-prem/edge complex (HPE, NTAP, HPQ, [[AAPL]], SNX, INGM, CDW) are the clearest incremental winners as you move RIGHT toward open-weights**, on the argument that *"more workloads are run on-prem or at the edge, benefitting hardware names."*
- ⚠️ **Scope caveat, stated because the note invites over-reading: Exhibit 2 is a scenario grid, NOT ratings, PTs or estimates. MS's own view is that *"evidence increasingly points to a multi-model world"* (i.e. Scenario 2), with *"the US/China model price differential"* the key swing factor.** Cross-filed [tokenmaxxing](tokenmaxxing.md).

## Recent signals (Aug 12) — 🔴 THE NEOCLOUDS PUT PRICES ON THE TABLE IN $/MW, AND THE MARGINAL COST OF CAPITAL BETWEEN HYPERSCALERS AND NEOCLOUDS HAS ARGUABLY CROSSED

**The two neocloud prints ([[NBIS]] pre-open, [[CRWV]] the night before) delivered the numbers this theme has been missing: what a megawatt actually rents for, and what it earns.**

- **🔴 DISCLOSED PRICING — the wiki's first hard $/MW anchors, from [[NBIS]]'s Q2'26 call (2026-08-12):** four landmark **one-to-three-year AI-cloud deals at $20–25M/MW/year**, versus *"substantially lower economics on older hyperscaler-backed contracts"*; **short-duration (three-to-six-month) capacity negotiated at $40–50M/MW and sometimes above**, with one already signed. **Payback on Q2 deals: 1 year 10 months, down from a 2–3 year payback previously.** ⚠️ **The ~2x term-vs-spot spread is the single most useful modelling input added to this theme this quarter. Corroborated independently by 22V Research (Dauvin Peterson, 2026-08-12) citing the same $20–25M and $40–50M/MW/yr figures.**
- **🔴 THE AUCTION PRINT, which is the cleanest scarcity evidence on this page:** *"In a market where we have several buyers for every GPU, we let the market tell us directly. **The auction result was a price 15% higher than we've ever seen before, and 20% higher than our pipeline for Blackwells**"* — for **GB300 clusters on three-to-six-month engagements**, driven by *"time-boxed, large-scale training runs ahead of a model release or reinforcement learning post-training sprints"* (NBIS Q2'26 call). And management is **withholding supply**: *"we could have sold out our planned capacity already today. However, we are choosing not to do so, reflecting our confidence with respect to future pricing dynamics."*
- **[[CRWV]]'s version of the same signal (Q2'26, 2026-08-11/12):** **~25% apples-to-apples price increases across SKUs on new contracts**, which management attributes **primarily to demand and customer ROI with component cost pass-through only secondary**; **contribution margins on Q2-signed contracts 5–10pts above the prior mid-20s average**; and an **A100 contract extending into 2029 on a SKU introduced in 2020** — *"increasingly, we are seeing longer utilization at higher prices."*
- **🔴 THE COST-OF-CAPITAL CROSSOVER — the structural argument, and it is the most important idea in this week's flow for this theme** (TMT Breakout EOD Wrap, 2026-08-12, synthesising the [[NVDA]] $500bn platform against hyperscaler issuance):
  - **Before:** neoclouds' marginal dollar was **equity — dilutive — with 9–14% debt available only against a signed contract**, so **only hyperscalers could build speculative capacity ahead of firm demand**. That was *"one of the pillars of their structural advantage."*
  - **After the NVDA-anchored $500bn pool (BlackRock, Apollo, KKR et al., priced at ~7–8% per the Altimeter post):** neoclouds get **(1) cheaper marginal debt (7–8% vs 9–14%) and (2) funding capacity to build BEFORE signing the customer.**
  - **Meanwhile hyperscalers are climbing the stack:** *"The ~5% AA/AAA rate is their average cost of debt, not their marginal one… **[[META]] showed the first crack: the ~$27bn Blue Owl financing for Hyperion, structured off-balance-sheet to spare the rating, priced around ~6.5%. But the decisive datapoint came in June, when [[GOOG]] skipped past debt entirely and raised $84.75bn of equity.** When the strongest balance sheet in corporate history chooses dilution and 6.25% hybrids over more leverage, it's telling you exactly where its marginal dollar is priced."*
  - **The conclusion: "marginal costs of capital haven't just converged, they've arguably CROSSED for incremental buildout — hyperscalers climbing the stack from 5% IG debt to ~6.5% SPVs to 6.25% converts and straight equity, neoclouds descending from 'equity or nothing' to 7-8%. Since the next gigawatt is priced at the marginal dollar, THE FINANCING PILLAR OF THE HYPERSCALER MOAT IS GONE."**
  - ⚠️ **THE PUSHBACK, recorded with equal weight and it is not weak:** the counter-argument is that **agents need the whole cloud toolkit (databases, storage, networking, security), not raw GPUs — and only hyperscalers provide that** — while **the labs' inference margins come from TPUs and Trainium being much cheaper per token than NVDA silicon**. ➜ **So the hyperscaler moat migrates from BALANCE SHEET to PLATFORM + CHEAPER SILICON. That is a narrower moat than before, but it is not nothing, and it is testable: watch whether enterprise AI workloads land on neocloud raw compute or hyperscaler managed services.**
- **⚠️ THE THIRD-ORDER CASUALTY, and it is worth naming: this same financing logic is now being used AGAINST merchant AI-silicon vendors.** On [[CEREBRAS]]'s Q2 print the same day: *"investors are increasingly worried about CBRS's ability to compete against the Big 3 of the AI chip market ([[NVDA]], [[GOOG]], and [[AMZN]]), who are increasingly utilizing not only technical factors but (perhaps most importantly) **financial might** to win deals — it's hard for CBRS to line up the type of $500B+ package that Nvidia just did"* (Vital Knowledge, 2026-08-12). ➜ **Vendor financing is becoming a competitive weapon in silicon, not just in infrastructure.**
- **Capex marks refreshed in the same week's flow (SIG NVDA preview, 2026-08-12): top-five hyperscalers expected to nearly DOUBLE capex in 2026; 2027 capex expected to EXCEED $1T (>40% y/y).** Per-name: **[[GOOG]] FY26 raised to $195–205bn from $180–190bn**; **[[MSFT]] ~$175bn FY26 vs prior Street ~$190bn — but the cut "primarily reflects a shift in data center leases from finance to operating leases," and MSFT expects >$50bn in 3Q (+45% q/q)**; **[[META]] $130–145bn, narrowed from $125bn+**. ⚠️ **Both MSFT "reductions" (the $36.6bn PP&E quarter vs $42.2bn Street, and the FY26 mark-down) are accounting/classification effects, not demand — carry the caveat with the number.**
- **Read-through to equipment, and it is a framework change rather than a datapoint: [[LRCX]]'s CFO raised the AI-capex conversion from ~$8bn of WFE per $100bn of AI capex to "closer to $9–10bn"** (JPM Mgmt Access Forum, 2026-08-12), citing **inference mix pulling through memory and CPU content alongside GPU/XPU**. ⚠️ **Applied to a >$1T 2027 capex number, the revision is worth ~$10–20bn of incremental WFE. Note this is an AI-CAPEX denominator and must NOT be substituted for the wiki's $7.5–8bn-per-GW card — different bases. Filed in `_meta/assumptions.md`.**

## Recent signals (Aug 10, PM) — THE FUNDING QUESTION GETS AN INSTITUTIONAL ANSWER ($500BN OF THIRD-PARTY CAPITAL), THE DEMAND QUESTION GETS AN ENTERPRISE-LEVEL CHECK, AND MICROSOFT'S SILICON RE-ENTERS THE CAPEX MIX

**🔴 (1) THE CAPITAL GAP IS NOW A PRODUCT, NOT A PROBLEM — AT LEAST STRUCTURALLY.** [[NVDA]] announced financing platforms with **Apollo, BlackRock, Blackstone, Brookfield, Goldman Sachs and KKR** to **"mobilize over $500 billion of third-party capital"** for AI infrastructure, on the premise that **"NVIDIA compute is an investable asset"** (NVIDIA Newsroom, 2026-08-10). ⚠️ **Read this page's standing funding-gap arithmetic against it: the point of the structure is that the capital does NOT sit on NVDA's balance sheet, and by extension does not have to sit on the hyperscalers' either** — it is an alternative to the debt issuance this page has been tracking. **The bear reading is unchanged and is about the aggregate, not the structure:** *"the AI industry in aggregate continues to have an **extreme paucity of cash flow**, and capital markets are being forced to plug the gap, but **investors can't absorb an endless supply of debt and equity in perpetuity**"* (Vital Knowledge / Adam Crisafulli, 2026-08-10). ⚠️ **Per-partner amounts, tenor, pricing and first-loss all undisclosed — carry as mobilization capacity, not committed capital.** The Street is convening on exactly this: **Barclays hosted "Analyst Access: A Cross-Asset View on Hyperscalers' Capital Raising"** (Ross Sandler, 2026-08-10). Cross-ref [ai-datacenter-power.md](ai-datacenter-power.md), [../NVDA.md](../NVDA.md).

**🔴 (2) THE DEMAND SIDE GETS ITS BEST BOTTOM-UP CHECK IN WEEKS, AND IT CUTS FOR THE CLOUD LAYER (UBS · Karl Keirstead, "Latest Enterprise AI Checks", 2026-08-10; ~15 enterprise IT execs at Ai4).** Two findings that bear directly on capex durability:
- *"Enterprises have pivoted from… **token-maxxing** to **token optimization**… **But at least among these enterprises, this has NOT yet translated into any pullback on AI spend, which remains strong.**"*
- *"We do **not** hear many anecdotes of enterprises scaling up their AI hardware and software stacks **on-premise** — their AI solutions are **almost always built and/or hosted on AWS, Azure and/or Google Cloud**. **AI is primarily happening in the cloud, not on-premise.**"* ⚠️ **This is the load-bearing assumption under every hyperscaler capex model on this page, and it has now been checked rather than assumed.** Also relevant to the applications layer: F500 enterprises *"**STILL repeatedly**"* express a preference to **build rather than buy** their AI products. Full detail on [tokenmaxxing.md](tokenmaxxing.md).

**(3) ⚠️ THE COMPOSITION OF HYPERSCALER CAPEX MAY BE SHIFTING BACK TOWARD OWN SILICON AT [[MSFT]].** The Information reports Microsoft *"plans to **sharply ramp up production of its next-generation Maia AI chips next year**, hoping to persuade **big cloud customers like [[ANTHROPIC]]** to use them"* (Aaron Holmes · Qianer Liu, 2026-08-10); **[[MRVL]] traded +4%** on it (BofA TMT desk / Brian Fenske, 2026-08-10). ⚠️ **Volumes, node, packaging and any Anthropic commitment are all undisclosed — the email carried the lede only.** Logged because, if real, it changes the **merchant-vs-custom split** of Microsoft's spend at exactly the moment Microsoft is reported to be compute-constrained on its highest-margin services. Cross-ref [custom-asic-tpu.md](custom-asic-tpu.md), [../MSFT.md](../MSFT.md).

## Recent signals (Aug 9) — the memory desk reframes the capex debate as a SHARE-OF-WALLET question: memory goes from <10% of CSP hardware capex pre-AI to 31% (26E) and 49% (27E) — and JPM concedes investors balk above 50%

A useful outside-in angle on this page's capex numbers: instead of asking how big capex gets, JPM asks **how much of it memory takes**, and treats the answer as the binding constraint on the memory bull case. (J.P. Morgan · **Jay Kwon** / Sangsik Lee / Neelay Kamath / Harlan Sur / Mio Shikanai, *"Global Memory TAM and S&D update"*, **2026-08-09**)

- **★ The share path (CSP-grade memory TAM as % of OMDIA-estimated CSP hardware capex):** **7% (2022) · 4% (2023) · 8% (2024) · 8% (2025) · 31% (2026E) · 49% (2027E) · 60% (2028E) · 47% (2030E).** Pre-AI the historical share was **<10%**.
- **🔴 THE HONEST ADMISSION, AND IT IS THE MOST USEFUL LINE IN THE NOTE FOR THIS PAGE.** JPM reports back from investor meetings that *"the generally **'above 50%' value share figure stood out as a STRETCHED LEVEL by investors**"* and concludes: *"we believe **ADDITIONAL HYPERSCALE CAPEX** (i.e. memory value share within CSP capex drops well below the 50%) **will be required for investors to view the AI capex trade thesis as more sustainable.**"* **Read the logic carefully — it inverts the usual direction of causation on this page.** The memory bull case does not just *benefit* from bigger capex; on JPM's own framing it **REQUIRES** bigger capex, because at a fixed capex pool a 49-60% memory share is not a bull signal, it is a crowding-out problem for everything else in the rack. **That makes the 2027 capex number a load-bearing input to the memory thesis, not merely a supportive one.**
- **The 2Q26 print that JPM says relieved the pressure:** combined **AWS + MSFT + GCP cloud revenue of US$106bn in 2Q26, +43% y/y** — with the growth rate *accelerating* from **+35% y/y last quarter and +23% a year ago** — and *"aggressive spending appetite"* into this year/next. **Consensus capex for the three CSPs plus [[META]] was raised 6% (2026E) / 17% (2027E) post-results.** ⚠️ The **+17% 2027E consensus raise** is an independent third-party corroboration of the ~+15pt 2027 re-mark this page logged on 08-03; different source, same direction, similar magnitude.
- **Why JPM thinks the share can be paid:** memory OPM settling at a **"new norm" of high-70%** (76% 26E / 78% 27E / 77% 28E) on a memory TAM of **$971bn / $1,442bn / $1,826bn** — i.e. the memory industry is capturing an unprecedented slice of the AI hardware dollar *and* keeping it. The counterpart is that **every 10pts of memory share is ~$150-200bn/yr that is not going to accelerators, networking or power** — the trade-off this page tracks under capital intensity.
- **⚠️ Basis flags before anyone lifts these into a model.** (1) The denominator is **OMDIA's estimate of CSP *hardware* capex**, not reported total capex — it excludes land, shell and much of the power stack, so the ratio is **structurally higher than a memory/total-capex ratio** would be and is not comparable to the capital-intensity series elsewhere on this page. (2) The numerator is **CSP-grade memory TAM**, not memory revenue booked to CSPs. (3) The **2030E drop to 47%** is a modelled mean-reversion, not a forecast JPM defends in the text. **None of these are adopted as house numbers.**

## Recent signals (Aug 7) — 🔴 A GROSS-BASIS ACCOUNTING TRAP OPENS UP INSIDE GCP, MICROSOFT IS REPORTED TO HAVE CONTRACTED 10GW YTD, AND THE FUNDING QUESTION DISPLACES THE ROI QUESTION AT ORACLE

**🔴🔴 (1) THE MOST IMPORTANT ITEM IS AN ACCOUNTING ONE, AND IT BREAKS CROSS-CLOUD COMPARABILITY.** SemiAnalysis reports that [[GOOG]] has a new revenue line inside GCP: **"SELLING FULL SYSTEMS OF ITS TPUs TO EXTERNAL SPVs THAT RUN DATACENTERS FOR CUSTOMERS SUCH AS [[ANTHROPIC]]. THESE TPU SALES ARE ACCOUNTED FOR ON A GROSS BASIS AT ~$35B/GW. In 2Q26, we estimate that TPU sales were AROUND $1.2B in the quarter, and thus CORE GCP GREW IN THE LOW 70s"** — against the **82%** headline (SemiAnalysis · Kan / Brookhart / O'Laughlin / Patel, 2026-08-06).
- ⚠️⚠️ **THE RULE THIS IMPLIES, and it belongs with the other basis traps this wiki tracks: DO NOT COMPARE HEADLINE GCP GROWTH TO AWS OR AZURE GROWTH WITHOUT STRIPPING GROSS-BASIS HARDWARE SALES, and do not apply cloud margins to that revenue — system-sale EBIT is "in the LOW 30% RANGE" versus total GCP "MID TO HIGH 30s." Roughly 10 points of reported 2Q26 GCP growth is hardware, not cloud services.**
- **Forward: "given OVER $150B OF TPU SYSTEMS BACKLOG, these TPU system sales will drive GCP's 2027 GROWTH RATE INTO THE MID 100s VS SELLSIDE CONSENSUS AT 64%," with "OVER $250B OF ADDITIONAL TPU BOOKINGS could be added to GCP RPO in coming quarters," worth "~$3 TO GOOGLE EPS IN 2027."** ⚠️ **Single source, proprietary model. Logged for the MECHANISM and the basis warning, not adopted as a forecast.**
- **This is the third instance of the same failure mode on this wiki, and they should be read together: [[CRWV]] defines capex on a PLACED-IN-SERVICE basis (not cash); the `estimates.json` CY-sum defect embeds pre-print consensus for reported quarters; and now GCP revenue mixes gross-basis hardware into a services growth rate. Different sources, same error class — comparing two numbers that are not on the same basis.**

YET, THESE ARE THE SERVICES THAT COME AT THE HIGHEST MARGIN AND REVENUE PER MW, BY FAR." Capacity serving OpenAI earns "~$14M/MW/YEAR" against a modelled "~$100M/MW/YEAR" for serving frontier tokens at API prices — a ~7x gap INSIDE the same company's own fleet.**

Two credible sources, same week, describing OPPOSITE capital-allocation regimes — one looking at 2026-27 orders, the other forecasting 2028-29. Logged as an unresolved tension, not reconciled.**

CUMULATIVE DEBT ISSUANCE COULD REACH ~$421BN BY 2028, YET REMAINS WELL-SUPPORTED. BALANCE SHEETS STAY VERY HEALTHY (EX-[[ORCL]]): NET LEVERAGE…"** with **"NET DEBT-TO-EQUITY RATIO PROJECTED AT JUST 18% BY 2029 DESPITE HEAVY CAPEX"** (@jukan05 relaying the same note, 2026-08-06). ⚠️ **The "ex-Oracle" parenthesis is doing real work and should not be skipped — the aggregate is healthy BECAUSE the most levered participant is excluded from it. Read alongside UBS's own Oracle note the same week: Oracle "will likely NEED TO ISSUE MORE DEBT IN CY27," with wider peer-deal and CDS spreads, though "the credit markets remain open" (UBS · Karl Keirstead, 08-05).**

Any aggregate that sums neocloud capex with hyperscaler capex is double-counting timing at best. The same caution applies in reverse to [[SPCX]], whose Q2 capex of $18.4bn versus a ~$13bn expectation is a cash figure.** ⚠️ **This page's own `## State of play` table should be read with that in mind; the note is filed here rather than editing the table because the basis for each row is not documented.**

Relevant to this theme only as a flow/positioning fact: the funding-short and de-grossing dynamics that have driven several of this month's post-print reactions now have a price-insensitive holder on the other side.**

Wistron's chairman Simon Lin: "AI SERVERS CURRENTLY ACCOUNT FOR 95% OF ITS SERVER BUSINESS and… the AI trend is so strong that EVEN HIGH MEMORY CHIP PRICES HAVEN'T SLOWED CUSTOMER SPENDING," adding he does not fear even higher memory prices because "CUSTOMERS WILL STILL BUY" (@dnystedt, 2026-08-06).** ⚠️ **"Customers will still buy" at higher memory prices is the single most useful demand-elasticity datapoint of the window and it comes from someone who invoices them — cross-filed [[MU]], [[DELL]], [[HPE]].**

## Recent signals (Aug 4) — ★ the Gavin Baker credit rebuttal arrives IN FULL: consensus OCF models Blackwell/Rubin monetizing at AMPERE rates, and contract-to-spot repricing is a ~$700bn swing on the credit need

**★ THE ARGUMENT THIS PAGE FLAGGED ON AUG 1 AS "NOT CAPTURED, ONLY ITS EXISTENCE AND DIRECTION" — @GavinSBaker's pinned "the market is OVERREACTING to hyperscale credit spreads widening" — IS NOW ON DISK IN FULL** (Invest Like the Best podcast · Gavin Baker, Atreides, ~2026-08-04; [transcript archived](../../_equity_calls/Overall/2026-08-04_GavinBaker_ILTB-AI-repricing.md)). ⚠️ *Informal podcast commentary — anecdotes and round numbers, not verified financials.* The chain, in his order:
- **① THE MISPRICING CLAIM, STATED AS ARITHMETIC: consensus hyperscaler operating cash flow (~"$1.3 to $1.4 TRILLION") effectively models the incoming gigawatts of Blackwell and Rubin "monetizing roughly at the rate of AMPERE — two generations behind. Not at Hopper but Ampere." Re-mark those GW at merely "a DISCOUNT to current Blackwell [spot]" and "it's more like TWO TRILLION of operating cash flow — and that kind of takes 700 BILLION of credit demand out."** Ironically the repricing then improves the credit ratios, making the residual debt EASIER to raise — the credit window reopens exactly when it's less needed. "Whether they choose to do that or not, we'll see."
- **② THE MECHANISM IS THE CONTRACT-TO-SPOT GAP, and it exists because everyone hedged the wrong tail: "I don't think anyone in 24 or 25 [bull or bear] thought that the prices of OLD GPUs would be going VERTICAL in 2026… everybody thought, hey, we're going to be smart, we're going to sign these long-term contracts" (and the neoclouds HAD to — offtake agreements financed the GPUs). "So essentially you have the contracted base of installed compute trading at a MASSIVE DISCOUNT to the current spot market, and as those contracts roll off compute gets repriced higher — AND SPOT CAN DECLINE and compute will still get repriced higher… essentially all the hyperscalers are UNDER-EARNING."** The week's datapoints: a several-thousand-GPU B200 cluster rented at mid-$2/GPU-hr re-upping ~7 months later at just under $4 (+50-60%, identical cluster); an inference cloud publicly planning to pay **~100% more** for Blackwells at contract expiry; Dwarkesh's $250k/yr H100 (~15x spot) — "that wasn't even in my considered-but-dismissed outcomes."
- **③ THE DOWN-PAYMENT IS ALREADY IN THE PRINTS: "operating cash flow from Microsoft, Meta and Amazon accelerated from 28 to 32 [% y/y]" — and ex "an unusual amount of one-times this quarter" (billions of legal items, "mostly fines to the EU") "we went from 28 to 35. That's a MATERIAL acceleration at this scale."** Before the Rubins land "at a meaningful premium," before the contracts reprice — and [[MSFT]] "brought on a huge slug of capacity in the month of June that didn't even show up in the second quarter." Consensus, meanwhile, "is modeling a DECELERATION, which I think is unlikely."
- **④ THE CREDIT FACTS, CONCEDED NOT DISMISSED: "real yields have gone up… spreads widen… CDS for everybody is blowing out," [[META]]'s bond "did not price where you would think" — "these are undeniable facts. And that would be really, really scary IF WE NEEDED DEBT TO FINANCE THIS BUILDOUT."** The buildout is still funded "overwhelmingly out of operating cash flows"; if compute reprices at current rates "you could probably fund ALL of it for the next several years." And the tail-hedge that makes the position convex either way: **"let's just say you do need credit to build the flops we need. Well, if credit's not there, it just means the flops that ARE there are going to be even more valuable"** — the capital-cycle discipline case, stated by a bull.
- ⚠️ **THE TENSION WITH THIS PAGE'S OWN NUMBERS, FLAGGED NOT RESOLVED: BofA (08-02) has capex at ~100-115%+ of OCF through CY28 with aggregate FCF margins −1% to −6%, and UBS Credit (08-01) has $275-285bn of 2026 public issuance with IG-tech spreads "showing signs of fragility" — but both are computed off the same consensus OCF that Baker says embeds Ampere-era monetization. If he is right, the denominator is wrong and the ~35-40% bond-funded share of capex falls mechanically as contracts roll; if he is wrong, spread fragility is the binding constraint. His own falsification tests, quoted: "if there was a pretty dramatic contraction in GPU prices that was SUSTAINED… that would be worrisome," and "if it started to get really easy to get GPUs" ("have you heard anyone say they have too many GPUs? Not a single person").** The arbitrating observable is rental-rate prints on contract rolls — the same one the neocloud pages track ([[CRWV]], [[NBIS]], [[SPCX]]).

## Recent signals (Aug 3) — ★ the 2027 CONSENSUS number is re-marked +15pts in a month, capital intensity hits an all-time high, and the ROIC question gets its first margin-level evidence

**★ ① THE CONSENSUS RE-MARK, WITH THE SIZE OF THE MOVE STATED (Morgan Stanley · Erik Woodring / Brian Nowak / Joseph Moore / Meta Marshall, "Global Cloud Capex Tracker: Spending Acceleration Continues", 2026-08-03).** **"2027 CONSENSUS cloud capex is now tracking to ~$1.2T, or ~30% Y/Y growth — 15 POINTS, OR ~$170B, HIGHER THAN PRE-C2Q EARNINGS," and MS's tracker moved "to 29% Y/Y in 2027, UP 15 POINTS FROM THE PRIOR FORECAST OF +14% Y/Y ABOUT A MONTH AGO."** ⚠️ **Basis note, because this page now carries three ~$1.2tn figures from three houses: MS's definition is explicit and broader than the big four — "Cloud capex includes capex from Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta Platforms, Alibaba, Tencent, Baidu, CoreWeave, Nebius, IREN, Tesla, Apple, IBM, and Oracle." That it lands on the same ~$1.2tn as BofA's CY27 (08-02) is convergence on the number, not agreement on the scope. Continue to carry ~$750bn as the big-four CY26 figure.**
- **MS SAYS CONSENSUS IS STILL TOO LOW, AND THE ARGUMENT IS ARITHMETIC RATHER THAN RHETORICAL: "We believe Consensus estimate's 29% Y/Y growth in 2027 — representing a 68-POINT Y/Y DECELERATION VS. 2026 — IS TOO CONSERVATIVE, as it implies NON-AI CLOUD CAPEX GROWTH OF JUST 7% Y/Y IN CY27."** Two supports: **MSe aggregate cloud cash capex is $1.4T, 17% ABOVE consensus**, and **"aggregate cloud capex estimates for the top 14 spenders has INCREASED EVERY QUARTER for, at least, the LAST 10 QUARTERS."**
- **THE INTENSITY NUMBER IS THE ONE TO REMEMBER: "overall CAPITAL INTENSITY IS NOW APPROACHING 35% based on Consensus estimates, A NEW ALL-TIME HIGH." And the re-marking speed: "if we go back to the beginning of this year, 2026-2027 combined Global Cloud capex was forecasted at $1.3T — EVEN LOWER THAN THE LATEST MSe FOR CY27 ALONE. Today, it's forecasted at $2.1T, or an INCREMENTAL ~$800B of spending, equivalent to 33% OF AGGREGATE REVENUE across CY26-CY27, ~4x THE 2014-2023 CAPITAL SPENDING INTENSITY AVERAGE."**
- **THE FOUR CY26 GUIDES, RECONCILED IN ONE PLACE — and only three raised: [[GOOG]] to $195-205B (from $180-190B) on "an acceleration in capacity delivery"; [[AMZN]] to ~$220B from ~$200B, "WITH MEMORY COST INFLATION AS THE CULPRIT," still flagging it won't be enough; [[META]] merely NARROWED to $130-145B (from $125-145B); [[MSFT]] underlying UNCHANGED at ~$190B, "though a LEASE ACCOUNTING RECLASSIFICATION (shifting from finance to operating leases) MECHANICALLY LOWERS THE REPORTED FIGURE TO ~$175B."** ⚠️ **Two traps in that list. (a) Do not compare MSFT's ~$175bn reported to the others' gross guides — use $190bn. (b) AMZN's +$20bn is attributed to INPUT-COST INFLATION, not to incremental capacity: it buys the same compute for more money. That is bullish for [[MU]]/[[SKHYNIX]]/[[SAMSUNG]]/[[SNDK]] pricing and neutral-to-negative for unit demand elsewhere in the rack, and it is the single most important distinction on this page for translating capex dollars into silicon units.**
- **ALL FOUR REMAIN SUPPLY-CONSTRAINED, from both sides of the demand: "GOOGL noting demand continues to exceed internal supply, MSFT reiterating that Azure demand remains above available capacity, AMZN stating 2026 CAPACITY WILL REMAIN INSUFFICIENT to meet demand and that MUCH OF ITS 2027 CAPACITY IS ALREADY RESERVED, and META expecting industry-wide compute availability to remain tight for the foreseeable future… demand is coming from BOTH external cloud customers AND internal AI workloads."**
- **REVENUE IS DECOUPLING FROM CAPACITY ADDITIONS, which is the mechanism that makes the ROIC case: "Cloud revenue growth is NO LONGER SOLELY A FUNCTION OF INCREMENTAL CAPACITY ADDITIONS, but also IMPROVING UTILIZATION, higher-value AI services, and expanding AI-driven consumption of broader cloud infrastructure." Azure +43% y/y with "incremental capacity MONETIZED ALMOST IMMEDIATELY"; GCP +82% y/y "driven by enterprise AI products, AI infrastructure services, core GCP workloads, and GROWING CONTRIBUTIONS FROM TPU SYSTEM SALES"; AWS +37% y/y, "its FASTEST GROWTH RATE IN 18 QUARTERS."**

**★ ② THE ROIC QUESTION GETS ITS FIRST MARGIN-LEVEL EVIDENCE, NOT JUST MANAGEMENT ASSERTION (Deutsche Bank · Brad Zelnick / Bhavin Shah, "Public Cloud & AI Update — Reflecting on C2Q Results: ROIC becoming more visible", 2026-08-03).** **The group print: "aggregate revenue came in ~4.5% ABOVE CONSENSUS with growth SURGING A RECORD ~10PTS SEQUENTIALLY TO +50% Y/Y @cc for the four largest US CSPs (AWS, Azure, Google Cloud, OCI). The record $14BN IN Q/Q NET REVENUE GROWTH was UP ~150% Y/Y despite ongoing supply constraints that are NOW SEEN AS LIKELY TO PERSIST THROUGH CY27 and putting a CEILING ON NEAR-TERM REVENUE."** **AI mix: "AI revenue now accounts for a MID-20s% of the overall mix for this group, up from LOW-20s last quarter and ~10% A YEAR AGO."**
- **THE ANALYTICAL MOVE WORTH BORROWING: "we'd point to the strong IN-PERIOD REVENUE OUTPERFORMANCE and upward revisions. We argue that CURRENT REVENUE IS MORE A FUNCTION OF CAPEX INVESTMENTS IN PRIOR QUARTERS, so OUTPERFORMANCE INHERENTLY IMPLIES HIGHER MARGINS/ROIC VS. EXPECTATIONS."** Direct margin evidence: **"both GOOGLE CLOUD AND AWS POSTED Y/Y OPERATING MARGIN EXPANSION (adj. for chip sales and a 1x derivative gain), and MICROSOFT'S INTELLIGENCE CLOUD SEGMENT OPERATING MARGINS WERE STABLE Y/Y" — with the unit-economics pillar stated: "although AI gross margins currently are LOWER than core cloud, there is VERY LITTLE OPEX REQUIRED to drive this accelerating topline growth."**

CPUs) and models… and also require ANCILLARY SERVICES SUCH AS STORAGE, DATABASES, GOVERNANCE, AND SECURITY CONTROLS." Evidence cited: AWS Bedrock, Azure Foundry, Gemini Enterprise, GitHub Copilot, Kiro.** ⚠️ **DB names the framing question directly: "the share of value captured at the MODEL LAYER vs. the SCAFFOLDING/HARNESS likely proves to be one of two key perception changes coming out of the quarter."**
- **BACKLOG, ON DB'S NUMBERS, AND THE EX-LAB VERSION IS THE IMPORTANT ONE: "aggregate backlog for the group ACCELERATED TO +194% Y/Y (from +183% in C1Q), and EVEN EXCLUDING LARGE [[OPENAI]]/[[ANTHROPIC]] CONTRACTS ROSE TO ~+63% Y/Y FROM +50% the prior quarter."** ⚠️ **That ex-lab acceleration is the cleanest available rebuttal to the circular-financing objection this page carries — the backlog is growing faster even after stripping out the two counterparties the bears say are round-tripping.** **Street capex estimates "revised up 10%/25% from a quarter ago" for CY26/CY27, implying +86%/+35% y/y in aggregate dollars, "alongside a sizeable Q/Q JUMP IN LEASE COMMITMENTS as well."**
- ⚠️ **DB'S OWN TWO CAVEATS, retained verbatim because they are the honest limits of the ROIC case: "C2Q WASN'T NECESSARILY AN ALL CLEAR, and RISING MEMORY PRICES HAVE ARGUABLY YET TO FULLY START FLOWING THROUGH," and "the FLOW THROUGH OF RISING COMPONENT COSTS VS. ANY POTENTIAL PRICING OFFSETS IS AN AREA TO MONITOR AHEAD HERE." CapEx budgets "look poised to absorb >100% OF OPERATING CASHFLOWS for the group in CY26/27 on Street numbers."**

## Recent signals (Aug 2) — the number gets re-marked to ~$860bn/$1.2tn, the MACRO measurement problem gets solved independently, and THREE OF THE FOUR BUYERS PUBLICLY DESCRIBE THE OPTION TO STOP

**★ ① THE POST-SEASON AGGREGATE, and it is above the ~$750bn this page carried on Jul 31 (BofA · Vivek Arya, "US Semiconductors: Spending More, Growing Faster, path to $1.2T in Cloud Capex", 2026-08-02).** **"Post Q2 EPS, cloud capex now approaching $860BN IN CY26 and $1.2TN IN CY27, UP +79%/+38% YoY from up +78%/+35% in early July."** ⚠️ **Reconcile the two numbers before using either: the ~$750bn on this page (JPM · Cardoso, 07-30) is the FOUR US hyperscalers; BofA's ~$860bn is "cloud capex" on a broader definition and its debt-financing exhibit references the TOP FIVE. The growth rates are nearly identical (+79% vs +80%), so the difference is scope, not disagreement. Carry ~$750bn as the big-four figure and ~$860bn as the broader cloud aggregate; do NOT treat the gap as a revision.**
- **THE BACKLOG IS NOW THE HEADLINE METRIC AND IT GREW FASTER THAN CAPEX: "capex is increasingly backed by multi-year customer cloud commitments and backlog… ACROSS THE TOP 4 CSPs, SUCH BACKLOG NOW EXCEEDS ~$2.3TN+, OR UP +16% QOQ, FROM ~$2.0TN LAST QUARTER."** Component parts: **[[MSFT]] $678bn commercial RPO (+8% q/q, "with ONLY 30% RECOGNIZED WITHIN THE NEXT 12 MONTHS"); [[ORCL]] $638bn RPO ("just 12% DUE IN 1 YEAR and 34% in 2-3 years"), plus "ORCL's PREPAID HARDWARE CONTRACTS FROM CUSTOMERS also reached ~$75BN, REDUCING THE CSP'S UPFRONT CAPITAL AND FUNDING PRESSURES"; AWS $496bn (>100% y/y); [[GOOG]] Cloud ~$514bn (vs ~$460bn in Q1).** JPM's version of the same aggregate is narrower and worth carrying alongside: **"$1.7TR across the big-3, with the backlogs rising +152% Y/Y," and "2Q NET ARRs FOR AWS, AZURE & GOOGLE CLOUD ALL SAW 2Q NET ARRs >60% ABOVE THE PRIOR QUARTER HIGHS"** (JPM · Matthew See / Doug Anmuth, APAC Thematics, 2026-08-02).
- ⚠️ **THE DURATION CAVEAT NOBODY ON THE SELL SIDE APPLIED, from the corpus: "Backlog mix to labs OVERSTATES their reality bc of DURATION ([[OPENAI]] is $138B but OVER 8YRS, ramping over time, all committed)"** (@SouthernValue95, 2026-08-02). **A $2.3tn backlog whose largest single components run 8 years and whose disclosed 12-month recognition is 30% ([[MSFT]]) and 12% ([[ORCL]]) is a very different asset from a $2.3tn order book. The right comparison is annualised deliverable revenue against annual capex, and none of the notes on this page do it. Flagged as the highest-value modelling gap on this theme.**
- **THE CASH TRUTH, stated plainly by the bull: "we expect hyperscaler FCF to remain pressured through CY28 as cloud capex rises to ~100-115%+ OF OP. CASH FLOW, driving aggregate FCF MARGINS TO −1% IN CY26 AND −5-6% IN CY27-28 VERSUS HISTORICAL +15-20%."** On funding: **"since beginning of 2026, top 5 hyperscalers have now collectively raised ~$270BN of capital, primarily through LONG-DATED DEBT (largely 3-40 year maturities) and permanent equity… we view these financings as B/S OPTIMIZATION AND LIQUIDITY PRESERVATION RATHER THAN EVIDENCE OF CAPEX/FUNDING STRESS."** ⚠️ **Note the tension with this page's own Aug 1 entry: UBS Credit raised 2026 hyperscaler PUBLIC DEBT ISSUANCE to $275-285bn while flagging "IG tech credit spreads have started to show signs of FRAGILITY." BofA's ~$270bn raised-to-date and UBS's $275-285bn full-year forecast are consistent on quantum and opposite in interpretation. The arbitrating observable is spreads, not volumes.**
- **PRICING POWER, from the buyers' own mouths: "hyperscalers ALSO POINT OUT THEIR CAPEX OUTLOOK INCORPORATES RISING INPUT COSTS (MEMORY, WAFERS, SUBSTRATES), i.e. SEMIS PRICING POWER."** BofA's five beneficiary buckets: **compute, memory, semicaps, power semis, optics.** Independent corroboration from the supply chain: **"higher capex guidance was expected given rising component prices, but IT SHOWS HYPERSCALERS ARE RAISING SPENDING TO ABSORB THOSE COSTS RATHER THAN CUTTING UNIT VOLUMES. That should ease investor concerns that higher component prices would REDUCE OVERALL SERVER UNIT PROCUREMENT"** (Morgan Stanley · Howard Kao, 2026-08-02) — **the single most useful sentence for the memory complex this week, because it says the cost inflation is additive to capex rather than cannibalising units. See [themes/hbm-memory](hbm-memory.md).**

**★ ② THE MACRO MEASUREMENT PROBLEM GETS SOLVED THREE WAYS AND THE ANSWERS CONVERGE ON ~$1TN GLOBALLY (Goldman Sachs Global Economics · Joseph Briggs / Sarah Dong, "Assessing the Current Pace of AI Investment", 2026-08-02).** ⚠️ **GS's opening point is that the number everyone quotes is the wrong number: "the most frequently cited measure is the projection for almost $800BN IN 2026 CAPEX FROM US HYPERSCALERS. However, this estimate IGNORES INVESTMENT BY PRIVATE AND FOREIGN COMPANIES and CAPTURES NON-US AND NON-AI INVESTMENT."** **Their adjusted build — excluding pre-AI investment trends, adding foreign and private AI-exposed companies, and allocating geographically by announced investment location — gives 2026 AI investment of ~$1,019BN GLOBALLY AND ~$582BN IN THE US.** **Two independent cross-checks land in the same place: "gross profit forecast revisions for public companies exposed to the buildout imply ~$1,060BN in global AI investment in 2026," and "a current investment pace of ~$1,002BN globally and ~$545BN in the US based on OFFICIAL NATIONAL ACCOUNT AND TRADE DATA."** **GS's summary: "AI-investment totals of $1TN GLOBALLY and JUST UNDER $600BN IN THE US in 2026."**
- **THE GDP PATH, which is the number macro allocators will trade off: "AI capex will rise from 1.8% OF GDP IN THE US (0.9% GLOBALLY) IN 2026 to 2.5% OF GDP IN THE US (1.3% GLOBALLY) IN 2027, with a further increase to 2.8% (1.4% GLOBALLY) IN 2028. THESE LEVELS ARE CONSISTENT WITH THOSE IN PRIOR GENERAL-PURPOSE TECHNOLOGY (GPT) BUILDOUTS."** ⚠️ **That last clause is the most important defensive fact on this page: at ~2.8% of US GDP the AI buildout is NOT unprecedented in scale relative to railways/electrification/telecom. It removes "the sheer size proves it is a bubble" as an argument, without addressing return on capital at all.**
- ⚠️ **TWO WARNINGS IN GS's OWN DASHBOARD, and both are new to this theme. (a) "A NOWCAST BASED ON TRADE DATA POINTS TO A MODERATE SLOWDOWN IN INVESTMENT (ALBEIT FROM A VERY ROBUST PACE) IN JUNE AND JULY" — the first hard-data deceleration signal logged here, and it covers exactly the months of the equity unwind. (b) "OFFICIAL US DATA IMPLY THAT RECENT INCREASES IN AI INVESTMENT HAVE INCREASINGLY BEEN DRIVEN BY COST INFLATION, suggesting that SPENDING INCREASES IN 2026 MAY PROVIDE A SMALLER BOOST TO REAL INVESTMENT THAN IN 2025." Read (b) against the MS/BofA claim above that hyperscalers are absorbing component costs rather than cutting units: both can be true, and together they mean nominal capex growth is overstating COMPUTE growth. Anyone converting capex dollars into GW or into accelerator units needs a deflator this year that they did not need last year. GS also cautions the GDP boost is "limited due to MEASUREMENT BIASES AND HIGH IMPORTED CONTENT of AI hardware."**

**★ ③ THREE OF THE FOUR LARGEST BUYERS HAVE NOW PUBLICLY DESCRIBED THE OPTION TO SLOW-WALK SILICON — this is the most consequential development of the week and it is a change in DISCLOSURE, not (yet) in behaviour.**
- **[[MSFT]] CFO Amy Hood, asked directly how Microsoft protects itself against overbuild: "You've seen our CapEx really pivot toward what I would call, and do call, SHORT-LIVED ASSETS, which really, that's CPUs and GPUs that have relatively shorter lead times… IF THE DEMAND ENVIRONMENT CHANGES, YOU JUST SLOW DOWN WHAT IS, IN FACT, THE LARGEST COMPONENT AND THE DRIVER OF COGS," and "WHEN YOU HAVE THE ABILITY TO LATE BIND SOME OF THE MORE EXPENSIVE COMPONENTS IN SHORT TERM, YOU HAVE A BIG BOOK OF BUSINESS THAT'S FLEXIBLE."**
- **[[AMZN]] CEO Andy Jassy, same week: "servers and networking equipment operate on a shorter cycle. We typically purchase these A FEW MONTHS BEFORE putting them into service, so we have strong visibility into customer demand before we trigger the spend. IF THE DEMAND ISN'T THERE, WE WON'T SPEND THE CAPITAL."**
- **[[META]] CFO Susan Li "notably LEFT THE QUESTION WIDE OPEN FOR 2028 as well, framing it as flexibility to scale compute up or down: SECURING LAND AND POWER NOW WHILE DEFERRING CHIPS AND OTHER BIG-TICKET DECISIONS"** (Redburn relay, 2026-08-02).
- ⚠️ **Tim Culpan's synthesis, which is the bear version: "data center land and facilities are only a fraction of the total outlay… because this all can be depreciated over 20 years, instead of five to seven years for server racks, THIS PHASE CONTRIBUTES LITTLE TO THE COST OF GOODS SOLD. IT'S BETTER TO BUILD NOW AND THEN JUST KEEP THE SHEDS EMPTY… This is A GAME OF CAPACITY CHICKEN, and no one wants to blink first. So executives are incentivized to PUBLICLY MAINTAIN THEIR LONG-TERM CAPEX TARGETS WHILE FIDDLING WITH THE ACTUAL AMOUNT OF COMPUTE THEY INSTALL QUARTER-TO-QUARTER"** (Culpium, 2026-08-02). **He cites [[SPCX]] and [[META]] both starting to offload their own compute to third parties as evidence someone over-built, and notes it is worst for [[NVDA]]/[[AMD]] ("book capacity years in advance… inventory on the shelf is poison") and for [[MU]]/[[SKHYNIX]]/[[SAMSUNG]] as price-takers.**
- **THE OPERATIONAL CONCLUSION FOR THIS THEME, and it supersedes the Jul 30 entry rather than contradicting it: reported headline capex was already a poor proxy for silicon demand (leases, useful-life extensions, shells). It is now a DELIBERATELY uninformative one, because the buyers have told investors they intend to hold the target constant and vary the install rate. ⚠️ THE OBSERVABLE TO TRACK IS THE SHELL-BUILD vs GPU-INSTALL DIVERGENCE — i.e. capex MIX (short-lived vs long-lived assets, which Microsoft now discloses at ~two-thirds short-lived) rather than capex level. A quarter in which long-lived capex rises and short-lived capex flattens is the signal; the headline will not show it.**
- **The counterweight, and it is currently the stronger evidence: all four raised 2026 capex this season, [[MSFT]] guided F1Q27 capex >$50bn from $41bn, Azure guided +45% on capacity that "continues to exceed" demand, AWS called 2028 demand "striking," and backlog grew +16% q/q. Late-binding is an OPTION the hyperscalers have bought and described. It is not a decision they have made.**

**④ A NEW COST LINE THE BUILD HAS NOT BEEN CARRYING — US STATES ARE REPEALING DATA-CENTRE SALES-TAX EXEMPTIONS, worth ~7%+ ON EQUIPMENT (The Information · Ann Davis Vaughan, exclusive, 2026-08-02).** **"Governors and legislatures in U.S. states that previously welcomed data centers are suddenly moving to rip away sales tax breaks that could RAISE THE PRICE OF EVERY GIGAWATT OF AI COMPUTING BY SEVERAL BILLION DOLLARS, potentially adding 7% OR MORE TO EQUIPMENT COSTS. In little-noticed moves that SHARPLY ACCELERATED THIS SUMMER, FOUR STATES ROLLED BACK OR PAUSED data center tax incentives, while officials in NINE OTHER STATES ARE CONSIDERING REPEAL measures."** **The arithmetic, using this wiki's own per-GW cost stack: "a gigawatt-scale campus requires roughly $40 BILLION IN IT EQUIPMENT, compared with $19 BILLION for the land, facilities, non-IT equipment and on-site power… In a state imposing, say, a 7% state sales tax, that $40 billion expense per gigawatt could grow by NEARLY $3 BILLION A GIGAWATT to roughly $43 billion."** ⚠️ **THE BIGGEST EXPOSURE IS TEXAS, WHICH IS THE MARGINAL GROWTH MARKET: "Texas… is supposed to DETHRONE VIRGINIA AS THE WORLD'S LARGEST DATA CENTER MARKET in power capacity by 2030, according to JLL," and Governor Abbott "in June suddenly directed the Texas Legislature in its next session to 'REPEAL SALES TAX EXEMPTIONS and other outdated or unnecessary incentives for data centers'" — with "TENS OF GIGAWATTS of AI campuses already underway or announced" there and the legislature taking it up when it reconvenes IN 2027.** **Others: Washington ended the exemption for REPLACEMENT AND REFURBISHMENT of DC equipment last month (~$207mn of tax by 2029) — note that specifically taxes the 5-year refresh cycle; Arizona paused DC sales-tax exemptions for three years; Virginia stopped short of repeal and negotiated a NEW TAX ON ELECTRICITY CONSUMPTION instead (~$600mn/yr collectively vs "$1 billion or more" under full repeal). Industry position per the Data Center Coalition: would "welcome the opportunity to work with the legislatures to put in more GUARDRAILS" pegging incentives to jobs and local investment.** ⚠️ **Why it belongs on this theme: an incremental ~7% on the largest line of the capex stack is a real ROI headwind that shows up in the CUSTOMER's economics, not the vendor's — it raises the hurdle rate on marginal gigawatts without reducing anyone's ASP. It compounds with the power-cost and grid-equipment inflation already logged here, and it is one of the "potential risks with future policy changes" an Nscale executive flagged the same day on an AlphaSense expert call. Timing note: most of this bites in 2027 sessions, so it is a 2027-28 model input, not a 2026 one.**

## Recent signals (Aug 1) — the FUNDING side gets its own number, and it was revised up ~20% in a week
- **★ THE DEBT NUMBER TO CARRY: UBS Credit Research has RAISED its estimate for 2026 hyperscaler PUBLIC-MARKET debt issuance from $230-240BN to $275-285BN — a ~$45bn / ~19% revision** (UBS · Sachin Ganesh, "Hyperscaler debt issuance", via Robert Ruple "Tech Musings", 2026-08-01). **The stated reason ties the funding side directly to the capex side this page tracks: "this reflects their expectation for FURTHER UPSIDE SURPRISES TO CAPEX and LIMITED FREE CASH FLOW CAPACITY."** ⚠️ **This is the missing right-hand side of the ~$750bn capex figure. Against 2026 aggregate capex of ~$750bn, ~$280bn of public debt issuance means roughly 35-40% of the year's hyperscaler capex is being funded in the bond market rather than out of operating cash flow — and the revision direction says that share is RISING, not falling, as capex outruns FCF. [[AMZN]]'s Q2 FCF of −$9bn against consensus −$1bn is the single cleanest illustration already on this page.**
- ⚠️ **AND THE MARKET IS STARTING TO PUSH BACK ON PRICE, not just on volume: "into this increased issuance, IG TECH CREDIT SPREADS HAVE STARTED TO SHOW SIGNS OF FRAGILITY. Despite recent weakness, MORE TECH SUPPLY STILL NEEDS TO BE ABSORBED (which should be able to happen IF PLANS ARE CLEARLY STATED AND THERE IS A PATH TO MONETIZATION)."** **That conditional is the whole thesis in one clause — the bond market is now underwriting the same question the equity market asked this earnings season, and it is applying the same test. It is also the mechanism behind the behavioural lesson already logged on 07-31: [[AMZN]] raised capex $20bn MORE than expected and rallied 14% while [[META]] raised LESS and fell 8%. "Clearly stated plans + a path to monetization" is exactly what AMZN's <3yr server break-even / 30-yr shell framework supplied and META's did not. The equity and credit markets are converging on one criterion, which means a hyperscaler that fails it now faces a higher cost of capital as well as a lower multiple.**
- **The debate this reopens, stated by the desk rather than resolved: "the widening CDS spreads (FT)" sit alongside AI circular financing, memory-cycle sustainability, token-maxing and open-source proliferation as the five overhangs "OVERSHADOWING A HEALTHY SET OF TECH EARNINGS FOR THE MOST PART" — with the honest caveat that "the narrative remains HIGHLY FLUID, with investors able to interpret the SAME DEVELOPMENTS as either a headwind or a tailwind depending on the mood of the tape."** ⚠️ **Counterweight logged, not adopted: Gavin Baker's pinned post argues "the market is OVERREACTING to hyperscale credit spreads widening" (@GavinSBaker, relayed via @ParadisLabs weekend reading list, 2026-07-31) — the specific rebuttal to the fragility read above. The argument itself was not captured, only its existence and direction.**
- **Sentiment context for how the season actually landed (UBS · Robert Ruple, 2026-08-01): "the MSFT and AMZN updates have SHIFTED THE NARRATIVE FROM AI SKEPTICISM TOWARD AI DEMAND RESILIENCE, AT LEAST FOR NOW." [[MSFT]] surged a record 16%, "adding roughly >$450 BILLION IN MARKET VALUE IN A SINGLE SESSION," on accelerating Azure, STABLE capex and resilient FCF — note that Microsoft was rewarded for capex STABILITY while Amazon was rewarded for a capex RAISE, which is only consistent if what the market is actually pricing is credibility of the plan rather than its size. [[AMZN]] delivered its FIFTH CONSECUTIVE QUARTER of AWS growth above 35% with a "measured increase in capex guidance." [[META]] and [[GOOG]] "were viewed as RELATIVE LAGGARDS among the hyperscalers."** ⚠️ **Tape asymmetry worth carrying: despite the rally, Tech/SINFT finished −0.54% on the week, SOX −4.3% 5D and the UBS AI Winners basket −2.09% 5D, while software (IGV) was +7.7% 5D. The re-rating was a ROTATION out of AI hardware into software, not a broad AI risk-on.**

## Recent signals (Jul 31) — the season closes at ~$750bn, and the market reprices the *narrative* rather than the number
- **★ THE HEADLINE NUMBER FOR THE SEASON: 2026 aggregate US hyperscaler capex now tracks to ~$750BN, up from ~$700BN prior — +80%+ y/y, or +$335BN in dollar terms (vs +$160bn in 2025 and +$95bn in 2024). June-quarter capex for the four was $170bn, +79% y/y** (JPM · Joseph Cardoso, "Cloud Capex Wrap-Up", 2026-07-30). Three of the four raised FY26; several signalled further increases for 2027.
- **The per-company reconciliation, and note that the STATED REASONS differ — which matters for reading through to silicon vs to cost:**
  - **[[GOOG]]: FY26 raised to $195-205BN from $180-190BN = 115%+ y/y, ~+$105BN — THE LARGEST DOLLAR INCREASE OF THE GROUP.** C2Q26 capex +26% q/q / +100% y/y to $45bn (~60% servers). Reason given: **"an ACCELERATION IN THE DELIVERY OF CAPACITY to meet growing demand"** — capacity, not cost. Significant 2027 increase reiterated. **Backlog +$50bn q/q to $514bn, the largest of the four.**
  - **[[AMZN]]: FY26 raised to ~$220BN from ~$200BN = +70% y/y, ~+$90BN.** C2Q26 capex +23% q/q / +69% y/y to $53bn. ⚠️ Reason given: **the $20bn increment attributed EXPLICITLY to HIGHER MEMORY COSTS** — cost, not capacity. **Backlog $496bn (+36% q/q, +$132bn).**
  - **[[MSFT]]: ~$190BN CY26 guide REAFFIRMED excluding an accounting change**; C2Q26 capex +29% q/q / +69% y/y to $41bn incl. leases, ~two-thirds on **short-lived assets (GPUs/CPUs)**. C3Q26 guided **$50bn+**; FY27 capex expected to grow y/y. **31 data centres added in the quarter (88 for the fiscal year), ~1GW, on track to double footprint in two years. Backlog $678bn.** ⚠️ **Effective FY27, MSFT is EXTENDING the useful life of data centres and office buildings from 15 to 25 YEARS.**
  - **[[META]]: FY26 NARROWED to $130-145BN from $125-145BN — a $5BN RAISE TO THE LOW END ONLY**, implying 90%+ y/y / ~+$65bn. C2Q26 capex +57% q/q / +83% y/y to $31bn; 1H26 $51bn (+66% y/y). ⚠️ **No reason given, but Meta had earlier attributed higher 2026 capex primarily to component and memory pricing — so the low-end raise may be cost rather than capacity.** Declined a 2027 figure, but framed plans around **"preserv[ing] flexibility for server decisions in 2028 and beyond."**
- **⚠️ THE MOST IMPORTANT SINGLE DATAPOINT OF THE WEEK, because it addresses the specific objection the July de-rating was built on: MSFT's backlog of $678BN grew +84% Q/Q — but +25% EXCLUDING OPENAI, with "+$51bn of the q/q increase driven by A BROADENING CUSTOMER BASE… AKA THAT'S REAL AI DEMAND FROM NON-FRONTIER MODEL CUSTOMERS"** (JPM Cardoso; BofA · Villani, 2026-07-30/31). Backlog growth is not just circular frontier-lab commitments.
- **⚠️ AND THE BEHAVIOURAL LESSON, which is the cleanest of the season: the market paid for ROI NARRATIVE, not for spending restraint. [[AMZN]] raised capex $20BN MORE than expected and rallied 14%. [[META]] raised capex LESS than expected ($2bn at the midpoint) and fell 8%.** Amazon's difference was a quantified payback framework — **<3 years to break even on servers/networking against a ≥5-6 year life with most AI capacity contracted for ≥5-year terms, and 30+ year shells yielding "at least five to six generations of server economics."** Investors called it a **"ROI Masterclass"**; Meta's print was judged **"META CONFUSION… vision remains unclear… TOO MANY OPTIONS ARE BEING KEPT OPEN? IT CREATES ROI QUESTIONS"** (BofA · Fenske/Liu via Villani, 2026-07-30/31). ⚠️ **Implication for how to read the next capex cycle: a raise is not automatically a de-rating, and restraint is not automatically a re-rating. The variable is whether payback is quantified.**
- **The flexibility argument, now standardised across two of the four: both AMZN and MSFT described SHORTER-LEAD-TIME assets as the adjustable portion of spend — Amazon naming servers and networking equipment, Microsoft naming GPUs and CPUs — so "spend can be slowed if the demand environment changes"** (JPM Cardoso). ⚠️ **Note the tension at MSFT: the flexibility claim rests on short-lived-asset mix, while the useful-life extension from 15 to 25 years works in the opposite direction by flattering reported depreciation.**
- **Proprietary silicon is now a disclosed revenue line at three of the four, and the accounting differs: [[AMZN]]'s chips business (Trainium/Graviton) at a >$25BN run-rate, triple-digit y/y, backed by multi-year multi-gigawatt Trainium commitments from Anthropic and OpenAI plus Uber and Pinterest; MSFT's Maia scaling internally and with OpenAI; ⚠️ [[GOOG]] BEGAN RECOGNISING TPU SYSTEM-SALE REVENUE FOR THE FIRST TIME THIS QUARTER, "with the vast majority expected in 2027"; and META framing its internal effort as "supply-chain leverage."**
- **⚠️ BASIS GUARDRAIL — THREE ADJUSTMENTS ARE NOW REQUIRED BEFORE ANY CLOUD-GROWTH LEAGUE TABLE HOLDS, and this is the single most reusable item in this block:**
  1. **GROSS vs NET revenue recognition. GOOGL's TPU system sales book GROSS; AWS books 3P model consumption through Bedrock NET. On headline numbers Google Cloud added the most revenue dollars q/q (~$4.74bn vs AWS ~$4.65bn) — but EX-TPU, MS estimates Google Cloud added only $3-4BN, BELOW AWS and close to Azure's ~$3.9BN** (MS · Brian Nowak, 2026-07-31).
  2. **MARGIN MIX. AWS added the MOST EBIT dollars ($2.5bn vs Google Cloud $2.2bn) off the SMALLER revenue add, because "TPU sales have a structurally lower margin." AWS/GCP EBIT margins 39%/36% in 2Q.**
  3. **BASE AND COMP EFFECTS + SERVICE MIX. GCP posted the largest acceleration (+21pp vs AWS +9pp, Azure +4pp) but off "a SMALLER BASE and a relatively EASIER COMP," and Barclays cautions that the IaaS/PaaS mix differs across all three so the growth rates are not strictly comparable** (Barclays · Raimo Lenschow, 2026-07-31).
  **Net: as TPU hardware sales scale into 2027, Google's cloud line increasingly mixes a services business and a hardware business at different margins — so revenue-growth comparisons against AWS and Azure will become LESS meaningful over time, not more.**
- **Absolute growth rates for the record: Azure +43% (guided +45% next quarter), AWS +36.7% "at much larger scale," GCP the largest accelerator. All three accelerated** (Barclays · Lenschow, 2026-07-31).
- **⚠️ THE DISSENTING VIEW, and the only house forecasting a peak: Redburn (Dom Ball) models [[META]] capex PEAKING AT $142BN IN 2027 and FALLING TO $131BN IN 2028 — explicitly "15%/30% BELOW CONS" — on an internal compute model projecting ~6GW OF OVER-CAPACITY BY 2030: "META HAS OVER-BUILT… a 'YEAR OF EFFICIENCY 2.0' WILL COME"** (via Falkiner, 2026-07-30). Logged because it is falsifiable and because no other house on this theme carries a declining-capex path.
- **⚠️ Off-balance-sheet commitments — logged as CLAIMS requiring verification against filings, NOT as datapoints: reports that GOOGL's SPV commitments total ~$826BN (rising ~$500bn/quarter) and that META "has already committed almost $700 billion in future AI spending," with aggregate hyperscaler off-B/S commitments at $3TN+, "double in one quarter"** (@zerohedge, 2026-07-30). **Unverified. Do not use without confirming against 10-Q/10-K disclosure.** A related concrete instance IS documented: **GOOGL reportedly providing a credit backstop plus chips for an [[ANTHROPIC]] 1.6GW / $15bn Texas campus** (WSJ, 2026-07-30).
- **Vendor-financed capacity is becoming a category, not a set of one-offs — worth tracking here because it decouples reported capacity from independent end-demand: [[NVDA]] is writing GPU BACKSTOP PROGRAMS for neoclouds (a MINIMUM REVENUE GUARANTEE on capacity they cannot lease out, in exchange for a share of revenue above a threshold), with FIRMUS and SHARON AI named as early partners** (GS · Murphy/Ng, AI Project Pulse July 2026, 2026-07-31). See [[NBIS]], [[CRWV]].
- **Beneficiary list per JPM: "Amphenol, Arista, Celestica, Ciena, Coherent, Credo, Fabrinet, Flex, Jabil, Lumentum, among others"** — see [[APH]], [[ANET]], [[COHR]], [[CRDO]], [[FLEX]], [[LITE]].

## What it is / why it matters
Hyperscaler capex (MSFT, AMZN, GOOGL, META, ORCL) is the single biggest **demand signal** for the entire AI hardware chain — GPUs/ASICs, memory, optical, power, semicap. Tracking the trajectory (and the gap between **consensus**, our **house model**, and the **street-high**) is how we size the cycle and judge whether the supply names are pricing too much or too little. This page is the synthesis layer over the AI_Demand_Capex dashboard.
