## Key debates

### ★★ (2026-08-05) THE RSI FRAME — the most complete BULL REBUTTAL to the July capex-bubble selloff this page has, and the reason to carry it is that it CONCEDES the bear case in writing rather than dismissing it

**A structured answer to the question this page has been running all month — "does current revenue support the spend?" — arriving the day after the Gavin Baker material above and reaching a compatible conclusion by a completely different route** (FUNDA "Deep|LLM: RSI Is the Most Important Variable, and Compute Is the Deepest Moat", 2026-08-05) [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Funda_on_RSI.html). ⚠️ *Independent research letter, not a broker note — no model, no price target, and several supporting datapoints are secondary. Carried for the FRAMEWORK and the scenario table, which are the transferable parts.*

**① IT STATES THE SKEPTICS' ARITHMETIC RATHER THAN DENYING IT, WHICH IS THE ONLY REASON A BULL NOTE IS WORTH THE TIME.** **"The four largest cloud providers guided to roughly $725BN of 2026 capital expenditure, up about 77% year over year, which absorbs the MAJORITY OF THEIR OPERATING CASH FLOW."** Measured against a payback period on current ARR, that — plus **STARGATE's $500BN AND 9GW PLAN** and **multi-track compute partnerships such as [[ANTHROPIC]]'s with [[GOOG]] (UP TO ONE MILLION TPUs)** — "STILL DO NOT CLOSE, which is precisely the calculation skeptics are running" (FUNDA "Deep|LLM: RSI Is the Most Important Variable, and Compute Is the Deepest Moat", 2026-08-05).
- ⚠️ **BASIS NOTE BEFORE ANYONE QUOTES THE $725BN: this page's own big-four CY26 figure is ~$750BN (JPM · Cardoso, 07-30) and its broader cloud aggregate is ~$860BN (BofA · Arya, 08-02). FUNDA's $725bn is a third-party restatement of the same four guides, not an independent count, and its +77% is inside rounding of JPM's +80%. Do NOT read it as a downward revision — carry ~$750bn as the big-four number and ~$860bn as the broad cloud aggregate. The genuinely net-new items in that sentence are STARGATE ($500BN / 9GW) and the ANTHROPIC-GOOGLE UP-TO-1MN-TPU arrangement, neither of which this page previously carried.** ⚠️ **And the 9GW is STARGATE's, not the 9GW of [[GOOG]] '28 incremental build already on this page — two different projects, never net them.**
- **The current-return leg of FUNDA's argument runs off the same prints this page already carries from the Aug-3 MS/DB entries (Azure +43% past $100bn annual revenue with demand exceeding supply; Google Cloud +82%; AWS +37%, fastest in eighteen quarters and its FIFTH CONSECUTIVE QUARTER OF ACCELERATION; [[AMZN]]'s ~$220bn "still isn't enough"). Not re-added — noted only because FUNDA builds on the same evidence base rather than an alternative one, which makes the disagreement purely about the FRAMING of the residual.**

**② THE REFRAME, AND IT IS THE PART THAT ACTUALLY CHANGES HOW YOU MODEL THE SPEND: capex is a DUAL INVESTMENT — real current returns from AI-driven cloud growth, PLUS an option on having sufficient compute when the RSI (recursive self-improvement) threshold arrives.** The asymmetry FUNDA draws from it: **"For each frontier player, OVER-BUYING IS THE LESS COSTLY ERROR,"** because a player without sufficient compute at that moment **"faces PROLONGED EXCLUSION rather than a late arrival"** (FUNDA "Deep|LLM: RSI Is the Most Important Variable, and Compute Is the Deepest Moat", 2026-08-05). ⚠️ **Read against the Aug-2 entry on this page where three of the four buyers publicly described the OPTION TO SLOW-WALK SILICON: FUNDA's argument is precisely why they described the option and are unlikely to exercise it. Both can be true — the flexibility is real and the incentive to use it is weak.**

**③ ★ THE CONCESSION, AND IT IS THE PARAGRAPH TO QUOTE ON A BUY-SIDE PAGE — a bull who separates "rational" from "profitable" in his own words.** **"Strategic necessity argues for the RATIONALITY OF THE EXPANSION, NOT FOR A GUARANTEED SHAREHOLDER RETURN… an arms race that is INDIVIDUALLY RATIONAL CAN COMPRESS INDUSTRY-LEVEL RETURNS, because when everyone must expand, THE COST OF EXPANSION GETS BID UP AND CONSUMES PART OF THE EXPECTED GAIN."** The rest of the concession, stated rather than buried: **compute assets DEPRECIATE, SIT IDLE AND BECOME OBSOLETE; GPU ECONOMIC LIFE RUNS TO A FEW YEARS; each platform generation pushes UNIT INFERENCE COSTS LOWER; and with TOKEN PRICES FALLING ROUGHLY 10x A YEAR historically, "a SUBSTANTIAL SHARE OF AI'S VALUE MAY SETTLE AS CONSUMER SURPLUS RATHER THAN PRODUCER PROFIT, as in previous general-purpose technology waves."** (FUNDA "Deep|LLM: RSI Is the Most Important Variable, and Compute Is the Deepest Moat", 2026-08-05)
- **The mitigant recorded alongside it, from the same passage: with inference demand as strong as it is today, PRIOR-GENERATION H-SERIES CHIPS RETAIN MEANINGFUL RESIDUAL VALUE AND UTILISATION** — "Over a full capital cycle, though, generational turnover's erosion of returns has to be counted." ⚠️ **That residual-value claim is the same observable Baker's contract-to-spot argument rests on (08-04 above) and the same one the depreciation-life bears attack ([macro-cycle](macro-cycle.md): Chanos on 11-12yr book life vs ~2-3yr economic life). FUNDA's "a few years" of GPU economic life sits with the BEARS on the accounting question while remaining bullish on the spend — a combination worth holding, because it means the two debates are separable.**
- ⚠️ **The consumer-surplus point is the most under-priced line in the note for anyone long the AI complex: it says the thesis can be RIGHT ABOUT AI and still wrong about who books the profit. Nothing else on this page makes that argument from the bull side.**

**④ ★★ THE THREE-SCENARIO RISK FRAME — reproduce it, because it is the only place on this page where a bull assigns his own risk levels to the capex he is defending** (FUNDA "Deep|LLM: RSI Is the Most Important Variable, and Compute Is the Deepest Moat", 2026-08-05).

| Scenario | RSI threshold | Implication for capex | Risk | FUNDA's view |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **A** | **~2027** | Compute runs at full utilisation, **strategic value is REALISED, and value CONCENTRATES IN THE ONE OR TWO LABS THAT CROSS FIRST and in their COMPUTE SUPPLY CHAIN**. ⚠️ **Even here shareholder returns are NOT automatic** — competitive intensity decides how much stays on the income statement vs passes to users through price | **LOW** | **Most likely** |
| **B** | **2029 or later** | Capability keeps improving **but a CAPEX DIGESTION PERIOD intervenes. UTILISATION DETERMINES WHO IS HURT FIRST**; depreciation and generational obsolescence steadily erode returns; **HIGHLY LEVERED PARTICIPANTS WITH WEAK CASH FLOW ARE FORCED OUT**; a meaningful portion of industry-level capital returns is competed away | **MEDIUM** | **"Warrants serious weight"** |
| **C** | **Longer delay, or continual learning unsolved for an extended period** | **The portion of strategic pre-investment that EXCEEDS GENUINE INFERENCE DEMAND FACES IMPAIRMENT**; the most pessimistic view is validated | **HIGH** | **Tail scenario** |

- ⚠️ **HOW TO USE IT: Scenario B is the one that matters for position sizing, because it is the only one FUNDA marks "warrants serious weight" and it is the scenario in which the SPEND STILL HAPPENS and the RETURNS STILL DISAPPOINT — i.e. the capex numbers on this page can all be right and the equities still de-rate. Its named discriminators are UTILISATION and LEVERAGE, which maps directly onto the credit leg this page has been tracking since Aug 1 (UBS's $275-285bn of 2026 issuance, [[ORCL]]'s CY27 need per UBS 08-05, the ex-Oracle balance-sheet caveat on Aug 6). "Highly levered participants with weak cash flow are forced out" is the neocloud/[[ORCL]] risk stated by a bull.** **FUNDA's own summary of the tiering: "COMPUTE DETERMINES WHETHER A PLAYER CAN COMPETE, which holds in both scenario A and scenario B. Whether competing proves PROFITABLE depends on timing, utilisation, depreciation, and competitive structure. We do not describe compute as the only variable, and we do not describe capex as a sure bet."**

**⑤ ★ THE STRUCTURALLY NEW DEMAND ARGUMENT — a THIRD compute category beyond training and inference: EXPERIMENT COMPUTE.** **Every production training run sits on top of a large volume of preliminary work — ARCHITECTURE SEARCH, DATA MIXTURE, HYPERPARAMETER SWEEPS, ABLATION STUDIES. "Fewer chips does not only mean slower training; it means AN ORDER-OF-MAGNITUDE FEWER ATTEMPTS, and the number of attempts shapes the pace of research accumulation." And the ceiling: "once AI begins PROPOSING AND EXECUTING EXPERIMENTS AUTONOMOUSLY, THE CEILING ON EXPERIMENT-COMPUTE DEMAND BECOMES A FUNCTION OF AVAILABLE CAPACITY."** (FUNDA "Deep|LLM: RSI Is the Most Important Variable, and Compute Is the Deepest Moat", 2026-08-05) ⚠️ **This is the one genuinely new demand leg in the note and it is unfalsifiable in the near term by construction — a demand category whose ceiling is defined as "whatever you build" cannot be used to size anything. Carry it as a reason the labs' own demand curve may not saturate, NOT as an input to a TAM. It is also the direct counter to Baker's sample-efficient-learning tail risk on [macro-cycle](macro-cycle.md) (if models train on ~10T instead of ~300T tokens, training's share of compute "asymptotes to something very small"): FUNDA's answer is that the freed capacity gets absorbed by attempts rather than released.**

**⑥ FUNDA'S OWN FALSIFIERS, written down because a bull who lists them is more useful than one who does not — and these are the tests to run against this page's numbers over the next year.**
- **RISK ONE — CONTINUAL LEARNING MAY BE FAR SLOWER THAN EXPECTED: "CATASTROPHIC FORGETTING REMAINS AN OPEN PROBLEM… there are NO MATURE ANSWERS ON HOW TO EVALUATE A CONTINUOUSLY CHANGING MODEL or keep it aligned through self-modification… if continual learning alone takes A DECADE RATHER THAN TWO OR THREE YEARS, both the adoption curve and the value of strategic investment shrink materially. THIS IS THE GENUINE SOFT SPOT IN THE THESIS."** ⚠️ **Author-identified, not our inference. It is also the same variable Baker named as his biggest scientific takeaway (08-04, [macro-cycle](macro-cycle.md)) — two independent bulls converging on the SAME single point of failure is the highest-value coincidence in this window.**
- **RISK TWO — CAPABILITY ADVANCES WHILE COMMERCIALISATION LAGS, and the analogue is priced: "The DOT-COM BUBBLE HAD THIS STRUCTURE. The judgment about the internet was CORRECT, and the judgment about TIMING WAS WRONG BY THREE YEARS, WHICH WAS ENOUGH TO LOSE 70% IN PUBLIC MARKETS."** And the tape read attached to it: **"the discussion of LEVERAGE UNWIND during July's selloff suggests THE MARKET'S TOLERANCE FOR THAT MISMATCH IS THINNING."** ⚠️ **"Right thesis, wrong by three years, −70%" is the cleanest one-line statement of the risk in scenario B and should travel with the scenario table.**
- **RISK THREE — SAFETY AND REGULATION DELIBERATELY SLOW THINGS DOWN: EVALUATION THRESHOLDS, COMPUTE CAPS, DEPLOYMENT LICENSING.** FUNDA's twist: regulatory deceleration **"also REINFORCES THE SCARCITY PREMIUM ON COMPUTE, since compliance costs are themselves denominated in compute and evaluation spend."** ⚠️ **File with the three political vectors already logged on [ai-datacenter-power](ai-datacenter-power.md) (NY moratorium, state tax-exemption repeals, the ERCOT queue audit) — those raise the COST of the build; this raises a ceiling on the USE of it. Different mechanism, same direction on timing, opposite direction on the value of already-installed capacity.**

## Recent signals (Jul 30, late) — the FY27 MSFT capex estimate now has FOUR independent apples-to-apples versions, and they only disagree on basis
- **★ THE FOUR NUMBERS, AND HOW TO STOP THEM LOOKING LIKE A CONTRADICTION.** Since MSFT's lease reclassification (finance → operating; ~$15B of CY26 spend moved out of reported capex; headline CY26 $190B → $175B), four desks have published an FY27 estimate and they range from ≤$215B to $260B. **They are not in conflict — they are quoting different bases:** **UBS ~$250-260B (OLD basis, capex incl. finance leases)** built off the implied **low-$60B December quarter**, and "exactly where most of the investors I was talking to were modeling into the print"; **Rothschild/Redburn $235-245B (OLD basis)** vs ~$205-215B as reported; **Wells Fargo $218-232B (CASH capex)**; **JPM ≤~$215B (CASH ceiling, backed out of the FY27 FCF-positive commitment)**. **The ~$40B spread between the old-basis and cash-basis clusters IS the reclassification.** All four reach the same conclusion — no change versus the pre-print bogey — which is why the semis complex traded the print as a reiteration rather than a cut. **BofA separately models FY27 capex +52% y/y.** (UBS + BofA callbacks, Wells Fargo, JPM, Rothschild, 2026-07-30)
- **The cash cross-check that makes the UBS number hang together:** UBS models **FY27 OCF $215B**, so "FCF modestly positive" implies **cash capex ~$200-210B** — against UBS's own **$209B pre-print cash-capex estimate**. Two independent routes (sequential build off the December quarter, and the FCF constraint) landing on the same place is the strongest evidence on this page that the reclassification changed the optics and nothing else.
- **★ WHY MEMORY INFLATION DID NOT SHOW UP IN THE MSFT GUIDE THE WAY IT DID IN AMZN'S — the answer is procurement, not denial (UBS callback with MSFT IR, 2026-07-30).** "**Microsoft already contemplated this inflation in its original CapEx guide, and… it has long-term hardware contracts that partly insulate it from spot memory increases.**" Keirstead's read on what the semis trade needed to hear: "semis analysts have been essentially yelling for some time that hyperscaler CAPEX estimates are far too low… **despite those huge spikes in memory spot prices, Microsoft's CAPEX was reaffirmed, not raised.**" **Set directly against [[AMZN]] the same evening, which RAISED FY26 capex ~$200B → ~$220B and attributed the increment explicitly to memory** — the two largest buyers, opposite outcomes, and the difference is contract coverage, not memory-price disagreement. That is the single most useful discriminator this page has for who eats component inflation.
- **The balancing caveat, kept because it is the honest one (UBS callback):** the CY26 guide not needing a second raise three months after March's material raise "**might have more to do with the fact that Microsoft is guiding appropriately than it does with any underlying market shifts**" — IR conveyed Hood "**still feels a bit burned from having to revise her capex guidance up in the past, such that she set it three months ago at an appropriately high level. That may be, frankly, the whole story.**" Also: IR said the "FY27 capex will grow" language "**was not intended to convey any material fall-off in capex post calendar-26**," and UBS reads the **material spike in total lease obligations disclosed in the 10-K** as a signal of continued high capex beyond FY27.
- **Morgan Stanley's cohort-level numbers, for the denominator (Brian Nowak, via "Playing the AI Infrastructure Dip", 2026-07-27):** "**we expect capex spend to continue to head higher (now expected to reach $1.2tr/$1.4tr across the five primary companies we track)**… we see this spend leading to more compute capacity… with **total hyperscaler available compute expected to approach 120GW by '28, up from ~30 in '25**." Per-name: **[[GOOG]] adds the most compute (9GW/11GW in '27/'28) but [[AMZN]] has the most available compute in '28 (35GW vs GOOGL 31GW); [[META]] reaches 14/21 total GW by '27/'28 from ~3.5GW at YE25.** [Source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Morgan_Stanley_700HK_Global_Thematics_Playing_the_AI_Infrastructure_Dip_Whe.html)
- **The physical cadence beneath the dollars (2026-07-30):** [[MSFT]] added **a full gigawatt for the third consecutive quarter** — 31 data centers in the quarter, 88 for the year — with UBS's rule of thumb that **each new gigawatt adds ~$10-15B of revenue**, and [[ORCL]] having added a full GW in its May quarter. [[AMZN]] is "on pace" to **double power capacity by end-'27 versus '25**, with the lion's share of '27 capacity "largely reserved" and "quite a bit" of '28 already reserved. Keirstead: the 1GW/quarter pace "**undermines a lot of the media stories about AI data center go-live bottlenecks… the AI infra industry is powering through those bottlenecks.**"

## Recent signals (Jul 30, evening) — [[AMZN]] breaks the pattern: an outright capex RAISE, and the increment is memory inflation not more compute
- **★ THE NUMBER: [[AMZN]] raised FY26 capex from ~$200B to ~$220B on the Q2 call, and attributed the increment explicitly to HIGHER MEMORY COSTS (AMZN Q2 FY26 call, 2026-07-30; MS · Thomas Wigg, 2026-07-30).** This matters more than the +$20B itself. Every other capex datapoint this season has been about *volume of buildout*; this one is about **price**. The same memory squeeze that took [[AAPL]]'s underlying gross margin to ~46.5% on the same evening is now visible as **hyperscaler capex dollars**, which means a portion of the 2026-27 capex step-up across this table buys **no incremental compute at all**. Corroborating: Bernstein's Rowan Adley notes [[META]]'s FY26 capex floor-raise ($125-145bn → $130-145bn) reflects "H1 actuals, better visibility and **likely some BOM inflation**" (2026-07-30).
- **The three-way divergence in capex *messaging* is now the discriminator, not the level (JEF · Rankie Wong "Reading the Tech Leaves", 2026-07-30; Bernstein · Tyler Seidman, 2026-07-30).** Same 48-hour window: **MSFT reaffirmed CY26 at ~$190B** on the prior basis (headline ~$175B is lease reclassification only); **META narrowed to $130-145B, raising only the low end**; **AMZN raised outright to ~$220B**. The market paid for MSFT (+9-15%) and AMZN (+8-10%) and punished META (-8-10%). JEF's read of the aggregate: "(-) On one hand, the **capex increases for 2026E so far have been relatively mild (GOOGL/META up +8%/+2% while MSFT kept unchanged)**, and both META and MSFT are sounding **more judicious about outer-year capex spend (opting for more 'flexibility')**; (+) But on the other hand, **hyperscalers showing some restraint on spend, while showing good ROI (thru accelerating cloud growth), with ballooning backlogs and strengthening demand signals** – all of which are +ve reads for AI monetization." Bernstein's framing of the same set: "hyperscalers **mixed to negative into 2H**" on capex optics but the cloud lines all accelerated.
- **★ [[AMZN]] gave the most specific AI-capex ROI framework any hyperscaler has disclosed — this is the answer to the July "unprovable ROI" de-rating (AMZN Q2 FY26 call via MS · Thomas Wigg, 2026-07-30).** Verbatim: *"Data center capital spent starting two years before. We can put servers into them to start monetizing … then get to monetize these data centers for **30 plus years** without having to spend that startup capital again. Servers and networking equipment operate on a shorter cycle … **If the demand isn't there, we won't spend the capital for servers and networking equipment.**"* And: *"**On average, it takes a little less than three years to break even** on that investment. The service currently has a useful life of **at least five to six years**, and **most of our AI capacity these days is being contracted for at least five-year terms** … For our data centers, which have thirty plus year useful lives, we should get **at least five to six generations of server economics**."* Two structural implications for this theme: (1) the **server/networking half of capex is demand-gated and short-cycle** — it can be switched off within months, which caps the downside scenario; (2) the **shell half is a 30-year asset amortised across 5-6 server generations**, so the "capex is all depreciation risk" bear case applies to a minority of the spend.
- **The cash cost of the above is now marked: [[AMZN]] FCF was −$9B in Q2 vs consensus −$1B** (OCF $45.3B less $54.2B P&E). Vital Knowledge: "the only two numbers that anyone will talk about are 37% (AWS FXN growth) and 39.4% (AWS op. margins), as both were amazing, but people should also talk about **−$9B … as this speaks to the cash pressures facing hyperscalers as they race to stay ahead in the AI race**" (2026-07-30). Compare [[MSFT]], which explicitly guided to remaining **FCF-positive in FY27** even as capex grows — the two largest cloud franchises are now on visibly different sides of the FCF line, and Bernstein's Adley notes MSFT's FCF-positive framing is only a **"soft ceiling"** anyway since it constrains cash PP&E, not capex including finance leases.
- **Demand-side: all three hyperscalers said supply is the constraint, and AMZN extended the horizon to 2028.** AMZN: demand outstrips supply **for 2026 *and* 2027**, 2028 demand already visible is "**striking**," and AWS "**can reach a trillion dollars**" of revenue over time; **backlog $496B** (from $364B in Q1), **AWS AI business >$25B run-rate** and the **chips business separately >$25B** (MS Wigg; GS · Eric Sheridan, 2026-07-30). MSFT: "demand continues to exceed available supply … every incremental unit of capacity or efficiency improvement is being monetized rapidly," with **commercial RPO $678B (+84% y/y, +25% ex-OpenAI) and the ~$51B q/q increase coming ENTIRELY from non-frontier-lab customers** (FundaAI, 2026-07-30; BofA · Fenske: "extremely bullish for the AI trade"). Barclays' Brendan Lynch published data-centre read-throughs off all three prints (2026-07-29/30).
- **Aggregate read for the AI supply chain (JPM · Albert Hung, 2026-07-30):** "Robust MSFT/Meta CY26 Capex outlook indicates a **strong 2H26 Capex uptick and bodes well for the revenue momentum of the AI server foodchain** in the coming quarters." MSFT guided **>US$50bn of total capex in the Sep-Q (+43% y/y)** with more finance leases reclassified to operating, which "implies an **even more significant step-up in cash Capex**." Barclays' Tim Long: **MSFT PP&E capex was $35.8bn in the Jun-Q, +110% y/y, ~2/3 on short-lived assets (GPUs/CPUs)** — names [[ANET]] "in particular," plus [[CIEN]], [[CSCO]], CLS and [[GLW]]. Confirmation in the tape: [[ALAB]], [[MRVL]] and [[CRDO]] all **+3-4%** on the AMZN print.
- **The counter-argument worth keeping on the page (@SouthernValue95, 2026-07-30):** "Capital markets are acting as bond vigilantes to stop perceived risky AI spending … **Risk to the AI trade is that capital markets slow the AI cycle to the rate allowed by hyperscale cash from operations growth** (which is still pretty robust). **This would be good for hyperscalers as it is a supply side constraint, unrelated to demand, making them the industry bottleneck and increasing pricing power. Bad for the rest of the AI trade.**" The bull rebuttal, also logged: GPU **spot** lease prices are said to be at least 2x contract prices, so hyperscalers are "underearning" on 2024-25 vintage contracts and cash flow re-rates as those roll (@QQ_Timmy summary, 2026-07-29).

## Recent signals (Jul 30) — the post-print rule change: reported capex stops being a usable proxy for silicon demand
- **★ THE STRUCTURAL POINT OF THIS PRINT SEASON — both [[MSFT]] and [[META]] now have mechanisms that flatter reported capex optics, so reported capex is becoming a WORSE proxy for silicon demand. Track cash PP&E / gross build instead (Rothschild & Co Redburn · Rowan Adley, 2026-07-30).** MSFT flatters via **lease classification** (useful lives 15 → 25 yrs shifts future DC leases from finance to operating, and only finance leases sit inside MSFT's capex definition); META flatters via **off-balance-sheet JVs** (the BlackRock deal). Adley's exact framing: "both companies now have mechanisms that flatter reported capex optics… so the metric to track from here is **cash PP&E / gross build rather than headline capex**, ie **reported capex is becoming a WORSE proxy for silicon demand**." This is the operative caveat for every row in the State-of-play table above — the 2027E columns mix bases.
- **[[MSFT]] — the desk split flagged on Jul 29 resolves as follows, and the apples-to-apples number is UP, not down (Rothschild/Adley, 2026-07-30; TMTB, 2026-07-30; JPM · Mark Schilsky desk, 2026-07-30).** Management's own words (per the call): CY26 investment expectations are **unchanged outside the useful-life adjustment**, and "the shift from finance to operating leases adjusts our expectation to **approximately $175 billion**." So: **~$175B reported ≈ ~$190B on the prior basis — a reiteration, not a cut** (~$15B moved outside the reported line). Forward:
  - **FY27 apples-to-apples ≈ $235-245B (+60-65% y/y); FY27 as-reported ≈ $205-215B (+40-45%)** — "the **entire headline reduction is classification**, so no evidence of any slowdown in intent" (Rothschild/Adley).
  - **Two soft-ceiling tells worth carrying:** (i) MSFT **omitted the word "significantly"** from its FY27 capex-growth phrasing versus prior wording; (ii) the FY27 **FCF-positive** commitment "creates only a **soft ceiling**: it constrains cash PP&E, not capex incl finance leases (which don't touch FCF at commencement), and the shift to operating leases actually **loosens it further** over time" (Rothschild/Adley).
  - **The same FCF constraint read from the other side:** if you accept Street FY27 OCF, FCF-positive implies **FY27 capex ≤ ~$215B — "definitely less than investors were thinking in the $250B to $275B range"** (JPM/Schilsky). Note this lands right on Rothschild's as-reported range, i.e. the two methods agree on the *reported* number and disagree with the buyside bogey. **Sep-quarter capex still guided >$50B.**
  - Schilsky's forward call, worth logging as a testable prediction for the whole cohort: "**MSFT won't be the last company to extend the useful lives of its datacenters and/or computing fleet. Mark my words.**"
- **[[META]] — the floor went up, the CEILING did not, and the house/Street marks are now spread across incompatible bases (Rothschild/Adley, 2026-07-30; JPM · Doug Anmuth, 2026-07-30).** FY26 capex $125-145B → **$130-145B**: a floor raise on 1H actuals, better visibility and "likely some BOM inflation" — and "arguably the more interesting observation is that the **FY26 ceiling did NOT go up despite component inflation**." No FY27 guide beyond maximising 2026-27 capacity with "more flexibility in 2028 and beyond." Gross-build arithmetic: **1H26 $50.9B spent → ~$87B left for 2H at the midpoint → $175B annualised exit run-rate before any 2027 growth → $180-200B (+30-45%) FY27 gross**; but the **BlackRock JV moves spend off balance sheet, "so reported could end up (potentially a lot) lower."** ⚠️ **Basis reconciliation for the table above — three numbers, three bases:** **$142B** (Redburn/Dom Ball, FY27 **reported** — which Adley notes "implies JVs absorb essentially **ALL** of the growth in gross build, an aggressive assumption off one announced deal, and the guide basis already includes finance lease principal, so JVs are the only lever getting you there"); **$180-200B** (Redburn desk, FY27 **gross build**); **$243B, +70% y/y** (JPM/Anmuth, FY27 — superseding JPM's prior ~$185B FY27E from "AI Capex 2.0" 2026-06-16). Do not average these.
- **⚠ THE REGIME CHANGE FOR SEMIS — "the era of 20%+ surprises to hyperscaler capex expectations is likely over" (Rothschild/Adley, 2026-07-30).** Based on the MSFT/META prints plus [[GOOG]] the week before: "**Semis demand signals are intact/arguably stronger, but no longer arriving as major capex raises from hyperscalers**," with revisions "getting smaller/two-sided." Adley's conclusion is the important one for positioning: that is "a **good read for hyperscaler ROI and cost of capital but removes perpetual upgrade/rerating fuel for semis**" — constructive on fundamentals, but "this choppy semis market could be with us for a while yet." Corroborated by the tape: JPM's APAC thematics concluded "**META & MSFT: results & guidance not enough to change the AI narrative**" with "Semis: unwind accelerates (again…)" (JPM · Matthew See, 2026-07-30), and UBS's APAC desk found "Microsoft and Meta **monetization diverged** on their implications for supply chain sentiment" (UBS · Randy Abrams, 2026-07-30).
- **Demand-side evidence that the intent is unchanged (all 2026-07-30 unless noted).** [[MSFT]]: **forward lease commitments $197B → $329B** — Adley calls this "probably the **cleanest forward volume signal**: these are signed, but not-yet-commenced leases, so **upstream of the classification change**, ie a purer demand read than the capex line"; **commercial RPO +84% to $678B**; **on track to double overall capacity in 2 years**; Azure +43% cc with the Sep guide at ~45% and demand still exceeding supply. [[META]]: **demand-constrained "for the foreseeable"** and **receiving premium bids for its compute** — and, notably for merchant supply, **it would rather sell _intelligence_ than raw compute**, which "helps keep the compute market tighter for longer if META decides not to play" (a positive read-through for [[CRWV]]/[[NBIS]] pricing). [[LRCX]] the same night raised its own **CY26 WFE call from ~$140B to the low-$150B range (+36-40% y/y)** on cleanroom pull-ins — see [semicap-wfe](semicap-wfe.md).

## Recent signals (Jul 29) — the first hard actuals under the bogeys: MSFT's FY26 10-K
- **NEW (2026-07-29, corrected 2026-07-30 post-audit) — [[MSFT]] FY26 10-K (FYE Jun-30, filed with the 4QFY26 print): cash capex $115.9B +80% y/y; cash + finance-lease basis ≈ $140.6B (computed); accrual-INCURRED ≈ $160B once the $26.7B of capex sitting in accounts payable (vs $6.9B FY25) is counted.** ⚠️ Basis discipline (this page's own rule): do NOT read the $140.6B against JPM's ~$140B FY26 model — JPM's series is reported-basis (~$88B FY25); the match is a coincidence of incompatible measures. Quarterly on the cash+FL basis: $28.5 / $36.2 / $34.9 / $40.9B. The two forward disclosures that matter for this page: **(1) datacenter leases not yet commenced $329.1B — 3.55x the FY25 10-K's $92.7B**, primary trail $92.7B (Jun-25) → $106.2B (Sep-25) → $155.1B (Dec-25) → $196.6B (Mar-26) → $329.1B (Jun-26), **+$132.5B in the June quarter alone**, commencing FY27–FY33, now with conditionality language ("with some arrangements subject to certain contractual conditions being met") — a milder, in-filing cousin of the LOI≠backlog rule this page logged 07-27; the pool is contracted capacity that sits ON TOP of whatever capex number the $260bn FY27 buyside bogey contemplates. **(2) a server-component RECEIVABLE program — "other receivables related to activities to facilitate the purchase of server components," $27.8B at Jun-26 vs $8.2B a year ago** (not a prepayment — MSFT expects repayment; the filing says "server components," not memory) — capex-adjacent balance-sheet support for the supply chain, OUTSIDE the capex line; plus **$11.3B of supplier-agreement-restricted investments** and **$34.6B committed for construction**. Funding: **zero FY26 issuance** (cash+STI down $18B to $76.8B; OCF $182.9B +34%) but an **entirely new cloud/AI risk factor** (absent from the Q1 10-Q AND the FY25 10-K): "substantial and increasing capital expenditures **and continued access to capital**… in advance of fully developed revenue streams" plus an explicit interest-rates/credit-markets/credit-ratings sentence — MSFT pre-staging, in its own disclosure, exactly the credit leg this page flagged on 07-28. Demand cover: commercial **RPO $678B +84% (+25% ex-OpenAI)** — duration shortening (2.5 → 2.3 yrs; next-12M 25% → 30%). ⚠️ The 4QFY26 call (07-29 AMC) then changed the reported-capex definition itself — useful lives for datacenters/office buildings extended 15 → 25 years + a finance→operating-lease shift that LOWERS reported capex (desks split on the rebased CY26 number: MS ~$175B / UBS Sector Sales ~$170B / UBS Research "$190B reaffirmed ex-change") — resolve against the transcript on [[MSFT]]. Full set + basis notes on [[MSFT]]. (MSFT FY26 10-K, 2026-07-29; audit corrections 2026-07-30)

## Recent signals (Jul 28) — into the [[META]] and [[MSFT]] prints, and the credit leg
- **The buyside bars going into both prints, from two desks the same day — and they do NOT agree on Meta's Q3 guide, which is worth holding rather than averaging.** **JPM (APAC Thematics, Matthew See, 2026-07-28):** *“for Meta, 2Q rev growth at **28% y/y** and 3Q guidance **19-25% y/y**. We don't know if we will get 27 capex guidance, but **the buyside bar in any case is $200-225bn**. For Microsoft, the focus will be Azure growth… with **41% growth expected for Jun-Q and 40-41% for Sept-Q**. On capex, **27 buyside estimates coalesce around $260bn.**”* **BofA (Claudio Franco, sales commentary, 2026-07-28)** collects a tighter Meta bar: **Q2 total reported revenue growth ~28%, Q2 GAAP EPS $7.53, Q3 total reported revenue guidance 24-25%**, FY26 EPS $33.37, FY27 EPS $36.20, **FY26 total expense guide $162B**, and a full capex ladder — **FY26 $147.1bn, FY27 $204.2bn, FY28 $248.6bn, FY29 $235.8bn** (note FY29 *below* FY28, i.e. the buyside is modelling a capex plateau/roll-over inside the decade). ⚠️ **The Q3 revenue-guide bar differs materially between the two desks (19-25% vs 24-25%) — flagged, not reconciled.**
- **BofA's own Meta estimates and framing (Justin Post, Buy, PO $835):** Q2 **$60.6B revenue / $7.50 EPS**, raised on stronger ad trends and checks (partly offset by FX). **He flags cost efforts as underappreciated — the May 10% layoff and job postings down 49% q/q.** Call focus: (1) content-retrieval and ad-revenue benefits from AI model integration; (2) usage and opportunity for **Muse Spark**; (3) **potential for external compute deals** (after press reports). The bull framing: **Family of Apps is a ~$250B “ARR” business at nearly 50% margins, and 2026 consensus revenue has been revised 52% HIGHER since the start of 2023 — the best total revenue revision in Big Tech.** The bear framing he concedes: the AI investments lack an obvious consumer-facing model, most talked-about AI use cases are B2B advertiser/merchant features, and **Meta is not a third-party hyperscaler (until the recent [[ANTHROPIC]] deal talk), so there is no obvious reseller business to justify the capex** — “concerns about thesis creep.”
- **MSFT setup (BofA · Franco, 2026-07-28):** *“investors are anxious to hear from Satya and Amy directly around their strategy with respect to **model/orchestration, open-source/weight**, their investment internally and externally in AI — **not just the capex guide**, but how they are thinking about integrating AI more effectively into the Office suite. **Azure expectations are very reasonable** (buyside pretty close…).”* Read with JPM's Azure bar of **41% Jun-Q / 40-41% Sept-Q**, the setup is one where the *number* is achievable and the *strategy narrative* is the swing factor.
- **⚠️ THE CREDIT LEG — the most important structural addition of this ingest. “Keep an eye on hyperscaler credit spreads… which are widening. There remains a lot of debate around if the hyperscalers are investing too much in datacenter capex — and whether growth is sustainable beyond 2027. **Previously, the hyperscalers just had to keep EQ investors satisfied, but following big debt raises, now they must keep BOND investors happy too.** Credit spreads though have started to widen (in isolation) — which **adds another layer of uncertainty around the ‘duration of growth' for AI capex**”** (JPMorgan · Matthew See, 2026-07-28). Corroborated on the tape the same day: **“hyperscaler CDS blow out to new record wides,”** printed alongside the **[[META]]/BlackRock El Paso, Texas datacenter campus** announcement (@zerohedge, 2026-07-28). **This is categorically different from an equity de-rating: it affects the cost and availability of the capex itself, not the multiple applied to its beneficiaries.**

- **Bull / where the money is:** demand visibility keeps pulling guides up (GOOG 2026 capex guided $75B→$175-185B→$180-190B over four prints; MSFT/AMZN/META all raising). Backlog (GOOG Cloud $462B, ~2x q/q, Q1 2026) underwrites the spend (Google official model, Transcript Dashboard).
- **Bear / what could break it:** ROIC/FCF — GOOG FCF went negative in Q1 2026 ($-26.6B) as capex/rev hit ~32% (Google official model, Cash Flow tab). The debate is digestion/air-pocket risk if monetization lags the build.
- Watch the **consensus-vs-house-vs-street-high spread** as the positioning signal per name.
- **NEW (2026-07-01) — buyer-only vs buyer-and-seller hyperscaler model:** Meta launched "Meta Compute" (established internally Jan-2026, publicly surfaced 2026-07-01) — an external cloud business to **sell** surplus AI computing capacity, with a roadmap to build 10s of GWs by 2030 and hundreds of GWs long-term (Citi · Ronald Josey, 2026-07-01). This is a genuine structural shift: Meta was previously purely a compute **buyer/renter** (from CoreWeave, Nebius, Oracle, Google Cloud, AWS); it now becomes a potential **competitor** to the neoclouds it has been a customer of (Barclays · Ross Sandler, 2026-07-01, flagging Meta's $20bn+ TCV deals with CoreWeave/Nebius plus multi-year Oracle/Google Cloud/AWS deals as the at-risk backlog). CoreWeave has ~1/3 of its backlog tied to Meta — headline risk to neoclouds if Meta stops signing new deals (Citi Tech Research call — Placeres/Josey/Radke/Rollins/Terry, 2026-07-01). Counter-evidence the "excess capacity" framing may be overstated: Google reportedly capped Meta's use of Gemini AI/Google Cloud due to Google's own infra constraints (UBS · Christina Dwyer, 2026-07-01), and Meta was simultaneously signing new capacity (Crusoe, ~1.6GW, ~2026-06-18) while capacity-constrained at Google Cloud in the same window — read as next-gen GPU compute still in shortage, with only legacy H100/A100 capacity being resold, e.g. xAI's Colossus 1 sold to Anthropic for $1.25bn/month, 220,000+ GPUs (JPMorgan · Wei Zheng Chan desk, 2026-07-01). Market reaction: semis fell 6%, memory fell 11% intraday on the capex-demand debate reignited by the headline, while Mag 7 was +1.6% and META itself +8.8% same day — JPM's house view leans toward positioning/de-grossing rather than a fundamental narrative shift (JPMorgan · Ellen Wang, Market Intelligence, 2026-07-01).

## Recent signals (Jul 27)
- **NEW (2026-07-27) — 🔴 THE ROIC DEFENCE OF THE $1.4TRN, WITH THE GW→DOLLARS BRIDGE THIS PAGE NEEDS — AND MS'S OWN CAVEAT ABOUT WHY REPORTED MARGINS WILL LOOK WORSE THAN THE MODEL (Morgan Stanley "The Paths to 25-50% GenAI ROIC" · Brian Nowak, CFA, with Stephen Byrd and Adam Wood, Internet | North America, Industry View Attractive, 2026-07-27).** **The anchor is MS's own 12 Jul 2026 work, restated here: "we detailed the path to $1.4trln+ of capex across the major hyperscalers… with compute capacity expected to quadruple from '25 to '28 (reaching an estimated ~120 GW)."** The whole point of the 07-27 note is that **the ROIC question — the thing "weighing on Internet/hyperscaler sentiment and multiples" — has an answer**, and MS builds three bottom-up models to give it one: **25%+ incremental ROIC, bullish AMZN, GOOGL and MSFT as infrastructure owners and GOOGL/META as model developers.** The full three-model token grid lives on [tokenmaxxing.md](tokenmaxxing.md); what belongs here is the bridge and the caveat.
  - **🔴 THE $39BN-PER-GIGAWATT DECOMPOSITION — THE BRIDGE BETWEEN THE GW NUMBERS AND THE DOLLAR NUMBERS ON THIS PAGE.** MS: **"we anchor to an all-in capital cost of $39bn/GW for GB300 as implied by our bottoms-up capex/capacity work, which flows through to ~$6bn of depreciation expense per year for owned infrastructure. Note that our cost per GW assumes ~$4mn per GB300 rack, consistent with our Asia semis' teams published rack BoM."** **The split: IT capex $23bn on a 5-year useful life; non-IT capex (powered shell) $16bn on a 15-year useful life; total $39bn.** Physical basis: **410,256 GB300 chips in 1GW.** **This is the single most useful line in the note for this page, because it lets any GW figure be converted to capex on a stated, sourced basis rather than a back-of-envelope one — and, run the other way, it says ~120 GW of '28 capacity is consistent with roughly $4.7trn of cumulative all-in spend at GB300 economics.** ⚠️ **Basis discipline as always: this is IT-load GW at GB300 rack economics, not facility GW, and it is a GB300-generation cost that will move with each chip generation. Do not mix it with the facility-GW marks elsewhere on this page.**
  - **⚠️ THE HONEST BEAR FOOTHOLD, AND IT IS MS'S OWN — WHY REPORTED MARGINS WILL RUN BELOW THE MODEL.** Under the heading "A Note on Reported Hyperscaler Margins," MS writes: **"While we believe cloud providers could see 60-70% incremental EBIT margins on GB300 inference capacity, REPORTED near-term incremental margins will remain below that range due to 1) the mix of capacity being served (majority of near-term incremental business is likely from prior chip generations, as GB300 capacity is still scaling), 2) mix of workloads (traditional cloud workloads in addition to new AI-specific workloads), and 3) additional opex that may not be reflected in this analysis (i.e. headcount and other R&D)."** **This belongs on the theme page in MS's own words because it is the reconciliation anyone will need when AWS/Azure/GCP margins print below the framework and the bears call the framework wrong. MS's answer is that the mix shift to GB300 fixes it over time — but the near-term gap is conceded, not argued away, and it is a real gap, not a rounding one.** MS is also explicit that the framework's virtue is generality rather than precision: it expects it to hold **"even as the new generations of chips and ASICs with improving performance arrive,"** while acknowledging custom ASICs and prior GPU generations have **lower costs but also lower pricing/hour and lower token throughput.**
  - **THE STRUCTURAL CONCLUSION FOR THIS PAGE, WHICH IS NOT SIMPLY "SPEND IS GOOD": "These healthy potential returns are also an invitation for competition, which speaks to how we should expect further new entrants/open source offerings to emerge…which will only further 1) keep an importance on product and compute efficiency innovation and 2) highlight the value of compute infrastructure assets."** **Read: MS is arguing the returns get competed away at the model layer and capitalised into the gigawatts — which is a bullish argument for the CAPEX OWNERS specifically, and is the cleanest available rebuttal to the "capex is value-destructive" framing this page tracks under Key debates.** The power/shell and utilisation legs sit on [ai-datacenter-power.md](ai-datacenter-power.md). [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/INTERNET_20260727_2024.html)

- **The primary source behind the MSFT bogey row in the table below is now on file, and the net-new content is the BALANCE SHEET, not the capex number (UBS · Karl Keirstead / Taylor McGinnis "Microsoft Corp. — Update on Azure, Capex and M365", UBS Evidence Lab, 2026-07-26).** The headline mark — **"we're raising our FY27 capex estimate FROM $234B TO $261B"**, with the **"investor bogey for FY27 capex… already $255-260b"** — is already carried in the bogey table below; **the prior $234B is the delta that was missing, i.e. a ~$27B / ~12% single-note raise.** Two more capex marks not previously on this page: **4Q/Jun raised to $41.8b from $41.0b**, and **CY2026 to $202b, ABOVE the company's current $190b guide.** **The dispersion UBS states explicitly is the more useful framing than any single point: "relative to the SELL-SIDE consensus capex estimate of $230-235B for FY27, our best estimate is that the INVESTOR consensus estimate is closer to $255-260B (we hear some investors as high as $270-$300B)… ~$25 billion above the sell-side consensus."** ⚠️ **The item this page did not have — what the raise does to the balance sheet, which is where the capex cycle stops being a P&L story: UBS's FY27 FCF estimate (OCF less cash capex) is "now almost break-even, at just +$3 billion," equity FCF yield collapses to 0.1% in 06/27E (from 2.4% in 06/26E), and net cash goes $49,906m (06/26E) → $1,299m (06/27E) → NET DEBT of $(13,408)m in 06/28E.** In other words the strongest balance sheet in the group is fully consumed inside two years at this spend rate. **On funding, UBS assigns a "low probability" to an equity raise while conceding the question is now live because of "THE GOOGLE AND ORACLE EQUITY TRANSACTIONS"** — the reasoning is price, not principle: **"Google did so at what was an all-time share price high. Microsoft shares are almost 30% off its highs and trading at a very reasonable 2027 non-GAAP EPS multiple of 17x… Microsoft's CFO will not be eager to issue equity at these levels."** **The memory-inflation decomposition is the cleanest number here for anyone converting capex into semis demand, and it is management's own: Microsoft attributed $5 BILLION of the 4Q/Jun capex step and $20 BILLION of 2H CY26 (annualised $40B) to MEMORY HARDWARE INFLATION — "a full year CY26 capex boost of $25 BILLION FROM MEMORY HARDWARE INFLATION ALONE"** against a CY26 guide of $190B, i.e. **~13% of the year's capex is price, not capacity.** That is the direct answer to the question Jefferies left open below ("a relative unknown is the extent to which the [GOOGL] $15bn capex increase is coming from higher memory prices, rather than… volumes") — at Microsoft, the split is disclosed. **UBS's set-up read, which is the sentiment mechanism this whole block tracks: "the Street is NOT REWARDING THE STOCKS OF AI INFRA PROVIDERS THAT SIGNAL HIGHER CAPEX SPEND and/or don't post upside to cloud revs growth and margins (SEE ORACLE AND GOOGLE)"**, and the expectation that **Microsoft "may need to inch up capex estimates AGAIN, MUCH LIKE GOOGLE DID ON ITS CALL THIS PAST QUARTER,"** driven by higher DRAM/NAND pricing since the last guide — while noting the hedge that **"Microsoft's sensitivity to spot memory pricing may be less than most think."** UBS stays **Buy but trims its PT from $510 to $480** (CY27 non-GAAP P/E 21x from 22x) "given weaker AI infrastructure market sentiment." (UBS · Karl Keirstead / Taylor McGinnis, 2026-07-26; Azure/token-optimisation leg on [tokenmaxxing.md](tokenmaxxing.md); see [../MSFT.md](../MSFT.md), [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md), [../ORCL.md](../ORCL.md), [hbm-memory.md](hbm-memory.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/ued48088.html)

Companion datapoint on how the last week alone moved: **sell-side capex for GOOGL "was raised 17% for '27 and 23% for '28 coming out of earnings"** — versus the ~8%/$15bn FY26 raise management actually gave, i.e. the Street extrapolated 2-3x management's own step-up. Filed here because the aggregate is the theme-level number; the per-name detail sits on [[GOOG]].
- **The financing-side sizing, from the bank arranging it: "$7.5 trillion in spending on chips, data centers and power in the next five years" (John Greenwood, Goldman Sachs global head of infrastructure and real asset finance, in a video discussion convened by The Information, "AI Financing Gets Creative," 2026-07-26).** Greenwood: he is "looking for capital in every nook and cranny" to support it, and **"the hunt won't end there, since much of that spending is on GPUs and other chips that need replacing every few years"** — i.e. the $7.5tn is not a one-time capital raise but a recurring one on a 3-5 year replacement clock. The Information's takeaways from the same panel (with Blue Owl's Matt A'Hearn and Skadden's Steven Messina): **"AI buildout needs $7.5 trillion, pushing beyond traditional data center financing"; "new equity partnerships fund data center development and hyperscaler projects"; and — the item to watch — "bond markets show 'digestion issues' after massive AI-related debt issuances."** Note the corroboration with BofA's number: $7.5tn over five years across chips + DCs + power is consistent in order of magnitude with $3.4tn of hyperscaler capex over three years plus the non-hyperscaler and power legs.

| **AMZN** F27 capex | **$300-350B** | **$277.7B** | ~$22-72B |

  Two things worth drawing out. First, **BofA's META bogey set is the only one on this page implying a capex PEAK — FY29 $235.8B below FY28 $248.6B.** Second, **R&Co Redburn's published META forecast of ~$145bn in BOTH 2026 and 2027, declining toward ~$100bn in 2028, is the low outlier against every mark above** and against Bernstein (~$220bn), JPM (~$200-202bn), MS ($175bn) and the house ($170bn) — and it is the capex path underpinning Redburn's new **$1,000 META PT**. Filed to [_meta/edge](../_meta/edge.md) as an open divergence; see [[META]].
- **The margin condition on all of it, stated as a contract — "we'll fund the capex so long as margins hold" (R&Co Redburn · Alex Haissl, "Margin Call" roadshow feedback, 2026-07-26).** The supporting ladder is the most useful new arithmetic on this page because it separates the mix effect from the level: **the Cloud 1.0 book earned ~66% gross margin; the all-in incremental cloud dollar today earns ~40%; and stripping out the traditional book, the pure Gen-AI slice backs out to just ~12-13%** — "well below the 30% floor investors treat as trough," with reported operating margins "resilient only because they're being propped up by aggressive cost cuts, not by improving AI economics." The MSFT-specific elasticity: **every 100bps of mix shift toward Azure Gen-AI takes ~77bps off cloud gross margin.** Haissl cites **Alphabet selling off on a capex step-up despite a revenue beat** as the validation, stays **Neutral AMZN / Neutral MSFT / Sell ORCL**, and gives an explicit trigger — **"when to buy? When capex guidance gets cut."** Two-sided and kept that way: the same note argues open-weight models are **"only 2x cheaper when you adjust for cache"** (see [tokenmaxxing](tokenmaxxing.md)), i.e. Redburn is bearish cloud margins *and* dismissive of the token-substitution threat.
- **Positioning into the prints, contradictory across desks and left unreconciled:** MS's EU client poll has **47% saying its $1.4tn 2028 hyperscaler capex forecast is "about right"** (25% higher, 27% lower) with **HFs near 12-month-low MAG7 exposure (10th %ile)**; BofA's platform flows show **LOs trimming MSFT/META/NVDA/GOOGL/AMZN since ~December 2025**; and Jefferies' desk reports the **worst week of Mag7 relative underperformance vs the S&P 500 in ~4 years**, with 4 of the 7 reporting into it. MS favourite-MAG7 votes: **AMZN 31%, NVDA 29%, GOOG 20%, META 20%, MSFT 0%.** Quick takes from the same call: META "highest earnings torque from capex disclosure," AMZN "the most crowded name," GOOG "at the centre of the model race but cloud/TPU confidence holds," MSFT "compelling entry but needs more product proof points," AAPL "back in safe-haven mode."
- **The bear framing that ties capex to the rate complex (Chris Wood, "Greed & Fear," via Jefferies Global SEMIS Daily, 2026-07-26):** the shift from **"A.I. excitement" to "prove the economics,"** with **the 10Y at >4.70% "already an amber warning" and >5% the threshold for real equity impact**; if the capex boom unravels he expects "an abrupt policy pivot from tightening to easing" and would accumulate gold and miners as the hedge. Jefferies' own semis desk adds the supply-side corollary: **"upward revisions to the capex spend of hyperscalers like Google are key to sustain the on-going strength in semiconductor demand… if capex spend were to start coming in line with expectations, this is likely to… trip the semiconductor supply chain into oversupply and a correction."** And the unresolved question that makes the semis read-through ambiguous even on a raise: **"a relative unknown is the extent to which the [GOOGL] $15bn capex increase is coming from higher memory prices, rather than semiconductor and equipment volumes."**

## Recent signals (Jul 24)
- **THE FINANCING THESIS, in full — the primary-source detail behind ">$7T AI debt by 2029" that this page had only in headline form (SemiAnalysis "Nvidia GPU Debt Backstop Unleashes the AI Project Trinity: Capital, Offtake and Datacenters", 2026-07-06; re-read from the raw source 2026-07-24).** The 07-06 note is already logged below in headline form (>$7T by 2029, ~$11.1T cumulative 2024-29, >$2T annual capex in 2028, the AI Project Trinity, NVDA's backstop and the $77.5B→$175.3B cloud-service-agreement path). **What was missing, and is the operative part for a capex model, is the pricing and the three gating obstacles.** **The constraint sequence, in SemiAnalysis's own words:** *"In 2025 … datacenter capacity was the bottleneck for AI compute growth. By early 2026, the datacenter supply situation improved considerably, but it became clear that chip production was now the limiting constraint. Now – mid-way through the year – it is clear that financing will now be one of the most significant obstacles."* **The three obstacles to the market actually reaching $7T:** (1) **hyperscaler backstops are not infinite** — "hyperscaler balance sheets will not be able to backstop trillions of dollars' worth of compute, yet outside the four corners of a 5-year hyperscale backstopped compute deal, **the appetite to lend drops off almost entirely**"; (2) **lenders are on the learning curve** — most banks have "a nascent understanding of AI Cluster total cost of ownership … tokenomics and end demand and still **hide behind the shield of an investment grade offtake or backstop**"; (3) **no risk-pricing toolkit** — GPU rental transactions are bilateral and non-public, and "**there is no active derivatives market to provide a pricing signal or a good reference for GPU residual value.**" **Pricing anchors (the numbers to model with):** current **5-year hyperscaler-backstopped deals price at ~SOFR+225bps (~195bps Z-spread, ~5.9% all-in yield)** versus **CoreWeave's 5-year unsecured corporate bond at ~10% (~600bps Z-spread)**; lenders size to a **DSCR of at least 1.3x** in the early years, "which typically corresponds to a **70-80% loan-to-value ratio**"; and the sensitivity that decides neocloud viability — **moving a top neocloud from a 5.62% to a 10% all-in cost of funding drops PBT margin from 14.8% to just 5.4%.** **Colocation price discrimination:** on a yield-on-cost basis (annual developer revenue as a % of project cost), **neocloud leases run 3-5% higher than hyperscale/IG deals** — the colocator charging more $/kW/month because the tenant's cashflow certainty and credit quality are weaker, and because the project debt behind it borrows at a higher yield. **The NVDA backstop sizing to carry into models: ~$5.9B of additional cloud-service-agreement contingent guarantee for every 100MW of compute Nvidia backstops**, on modelled backstopped capacity of **932MW (F1/27, of which 432MW announced), 1,000MW (F1/28) and 1,500MW (F1/29)** — and neocloud IRRs of **25.4% on a 1-year rental book under the backstop vs 40.7% unbackstopped**, going to zero-or-negative if the backstop is actually activated (deliberate: lenders are covered, the operator is not made complacent). Read for this page: **the capex table above is now gated by a credit market that does not yet have a price index, a residual-value reference, or a lending template beyond the IG-backstopped 5-year deal** — which is precisely why "who funds it" replaced "is demand real" as the theme's centre of gravity. (SemiAnalysis, 2026-07-06; see [../NVDA.md](../NVDA.md), [../CRWV.md](../CRWV.md), [ai-datacenter-power.md](ai-datacenter-power.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_06_ENC_Nvidia_GPU_Debt_Backstop_Unleashes_the_AI_Project_Trinity_Capital_Offtake_and_Datacenters_email.html)
- **The SpaceX-created market segment, and why only Oracle and Meta can play it — the piece of the 07-02 note not yet on this page (SemiAnalysis "Meta Compute: Everyone Wants To Be A Cloud", 2026-07-02; re-read 2026-07-24).** The >5GW/1H26, ~10GW-since-early-2024, 2.5GW-in-two-campuses and "2027 capex shockingly high" marks are already logged in the Jul 6 block below. **Net-new here is the deal structure that reprices the whole rental market:** SpaceX's Anthropic and Google compute deals are priced at **"triple and quadruple what peers are charging" on revenue per MW** — with the profit-per-MW gap even larger since the cost structure is roughly the same — and the tenor is unprecedented: *"the deal is three years, but with option to cancel from both parties within 90 days – so it's effectively a 3-month deal with automatic renewal."* SemiAnalysis: this "effectively leaves only two companies that can truly capitalize on this new market: **Oracle and Meta**," and for [../ORCL.md](../ORCL.md) it reads as **"a massive blow… yet another piece of evidence that they could've done a much better job at monetizing all of their gigawatts of compute,"** illustrated by the Oracle-vs-SpaceX valuation divergence. For [../META.md](../META.md) the arithmetic is trivially attractive: **at ~$50B of annual revenue per GW, allocating just 200MW externally drives ~$10B/yr at sky-high margin**, and the 90-day cancellation means Meta can claw the compute back for MSL on short notice — a call option, not a commitment. The symmetric point SemiAnalysis flags: the 90-day bilateral cancellation was read as a win for the *renters* (Anthropic/Google can leave if they find cheaper compute) but it equally lets the *seller* reclaim capacity. Read for this page: **a high-price, short-tenor segment now exists alongside the 5-year IG-backstopped template** — which is exactly the tenor the financing market (bullet above) cannot yet lend against. (SemiAnalysis "Meta Compute: Everyone Wants To Be A Cloud", 2026-07-02; SpaceX = SPCX) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_02_ENC_Meta_Compute_Everyone_Wants_To_Be_A_Cloud_email.html)
- **BACKFILL — HISTORICAL, dated 2026-04-20 (ingested 2026-07-24; ~3 months old, pricing inputs an Aug-2025 snapshot) — the build-versus-rent leg, quantified: hyperscaler-owned capacity costs 1.10x-1.61x a gold-tier neocloud on a like-for-like cluster TCO basis (SemiAnalysis "How Much Do GPU Clusters Really Cost?", 2026-04-20).** Directly relevant to the capex table above, because it prices the alternative to spending. Holding $/GPU-hr constant and building full cluster TCO (GPU + storage + network + control plane + support + goodput + setup + debugging) over a 3-year term, SemiAnalysis finds the **hyperscaler is the most expensive option in two of three workloads**: **5,184 GB300 NVL72 large-LLM pretrain → gold-tier neocloud 1.00x / hyperscaler 1.10x / silver-tier 1.15x** (the hyperscaler's 10% premium is support plus EFA performance tuning — a modelled 1-month paid POC plus one week a month of two engineers on ongoing network debugging at $200k/engineer); **2,048 B200 multimodal RL research → 1.00x / hyperscaler 1.61x / 1.15x** (GPU price $3.10 vs $2.40, a ~10% orchestration premium for slurm/k8s, plus storage and setup); **512 H200 inference endpoints → hyperscaler 1.59x**. Also relevant: a **gold-tier neocloud is 5-15% cheaper on TCO than a silver-tier one** at equal sticker price, so tier quality — not just price — is a real cost line. Read for this page: the marginal gigawatt is not obviously cheaper in-house, which is the analytical basis for the "bulk of additions now come through third parties" pattern the Meta/SemiAnalysis bullets below describe — and for Barclays' 07-24 argument above that asset-light structures could *reduce* future capex intensity. (SemiAnalysis "How Much Do GPU Clusters Really Cost?", 2026-04-20 — **historical backfill, Aug-2025 price inputs; GPU rental prices were rising as of April 2026**; the goodput/margin detail is on [tokenmaxxing.md](tokenmaxxing.md)) [source](../../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_04_20_FW_How_Much_Do_GPU_Clusters_Really_Cost_email.html)
- **The market repriced the capex cycle — and the pushback is now about who pays, not whether demand is real.** The tape on 07-23: **GOOG -6.9%, TSLA -14.5%, SPX -1.2%, NDX -1.9%, SOX -54bps, IGV -2.2%**, with **Asian memory very weak and the KOSPI -5.7%** overnight on no memory-specific newsflow (Rothschild & Co · Adley, 2026-07-24). CNBC framed it directly: Alphabet and Tesla fell "after both firms signalled increased AI spending, **unnerving investors worried about the mounting costs of the artificial intelligence boom**"; the FT led with "**Google burning through cash for the first time since going public decades ago**." The credit-market expression is worth recording: **7 of the 10 most-shorted bonds in the IG market are hyperscalers, with the 6%+ AA GOOGL 56s the single most shorted bond** (@zerohedge, 2026-07-22).
- **Buy-side positioning after the [../GOOG.md](../GOOG.md) print (Barclays desk · Justin Kantrowitz, 2026-07-24, NOT research):** **"most investors expect the stock to be a source of funds in the near term as we get past the hurdle of seeing negative FCF and knowing that's going to worsen"** — decent long-only two-way flow with **some rotation into AMZN**; the AMZN/META sympathy moves were "not expected." **One mechanism floated and then debunked:** that funds long GOOGL equity *and* heavy in hyperscaler debt were trimming the equity leg — Kantrowitz **polled four long-only investors, all of whom said no such dynamic existed at their firm**. His own counter-framing: investors "seem to only focus on the balance sheet impact and seem to [be] **missing the potential ramp in revenues that are coming**."
- **The revenue-side rebuttal, quantified for the first time: Barclays models ~$250B of 2028 TPU-as-a-Service revenue and argues the asset-light structure may REVERSE some future capex growth** (Barclays · Ross Sandler, "Unpacking TPU-As-A-Service", 2026-07-24) — including potential **downside to Barclays' own $500B '28 Alphabet capex estimate** as merchant TPU sales improve capital efficiency. This is the first argument in this theme that a hyperscaler's AI build-out could become **less** capex-intensive rather than more. Consolidated revisions: Alphabet revenue **+12% to $650B '27 / +40% to $950B '28**; **EPS FY27 $15.40 (from $13.94), FY28 $21.88 (from $15.98)**; **PT $425 held in level but re-struck on a 2027 multiple.** Semis-side companion: **~10GW deployed CY27 → >20GW CY28 at ~$15B/GW = ~$150B/>$300B of AI semis revenue vs Street ~$120B** (Barclays · Tom O'Malley), with the note that **"the method of sales to end customers matters less than production numbers."** See [custom-asic-tpu](custom-asic-tpu.md), [../AVGO.md](../AVGO.md).
- **Memory is where hyperscaler capex converts most mechanically — and Google alone needs 50%+ more of it (BofA · Justin Post via Simon Woo, "Global Memory Tech", 2026-07-23/24).** Post models **Alphabet capex US$200bn/300bn in 2026/27 vs US$91bn in 2025**, implying **Google needs 50%+ additional memory chips in 2027 relative to 2026** — via memory's rising share of Big Tech capex, **LTA-governed DRAM/NAND prices unlikely to rise much from 4Q26/1Q27 (so more bits at stable ASPs)**, and **external TPU sales pulling more Asian memory including HBM**. Post's FCF path for Alphabet is notably less alarming than the tape implies: **-US$16bn/-18bn in 2026/27, rebounding to +US$37bn thereafter**, with ~US$50bn of cash p.a. through 2026-28. See [hbm-memory](hbm-memory.md).
- **The non-hyperscaler spenders raised too — and one of them is now the largest single forward commitment in this theme.** **[../OPENAI.md](../OPENAI.md) raised its 2030 capex forecast to ~$750bn** (@Hedgeye, 2026-07-22; corroborated by MS TMT · Wigg, 07-22), with Sam Altman framing the binding constraint as inference: *"we have not invested enough in delivering cheap intelligence at scale. **all of frontier labs are gonna be inference companies** to a certain degree."* Consistent with [../AMD.md](../AMD.md)'s event datapoint that **inference now exceeds training at ~60% of AI compute in 2026** on **~35 quadrillion monthly tokens (~160x growth in two years)**. **[../INTC.md](../INTC.md) also joined the capex-raisers** — FY26 to **>$20B** with CY27 "significantly above" — while conceding **2027 positive FCF is "more challenged"** and that it **"may need to tap the capital markets"** (see [semicap-wfe](semicap-wfe.md)). **The pattern across this whole week's prints is the same shape: demand confirmed, spending raised, funding unresolved.**
- ⚠️ **The one datapoint that would undercut the entire chain, and it is unverified:** Rothschild's desk reports **TickerTrends data suggesting Anthropic ARR sequential growth has slowed significantly** — carried with the analyst's own caveat ("I have no idea if this data is legit") and the reason it matters: **"an air pocket in Frontier Labs growth has been exactly what the market has been worrying about (the open vs closed-source debate)"** (Rothschild & Co · Adley, 2026-07-24). Anthropic is the anchor customer for Google's TPU-aaS build, a 2GW AMD commitment, ~5GW at AWS and ~1GW at Azure — so if this is real it is a demand-side question for the whole theme, not one name. **Nothing else on this page corroborates it** (Yipit had ARR at $69B on 07-10 with daily adds accelerating; SemiAnalysis >$60B on 07-08). Flagged as the top item to verify at the next hard datapoint. See [../ANTHROPIC.md](../ANTHROPIC.md).
